-CAPITAL" CHAT.

The split in the Irish Unionist ranks which has led to eu much delay in the appointment of the Committee on the Land Acts still continues, and lends, I hear, to become worse rather than better. It is said to be now quite an open question whether either Mr. T. W. Russell or :sir T. Lea—the two UlsteraLiberal Unionists—will again contest their seats. In point of fact it is practically certain that neither of them will do so unless affairs undergo an entire change. Sir Thomas Lea is in an indifferent state of health ; but apart from this the strained re- lations between the landlords and tenant farmers in Ulster have rendered the position very nearly in- tolerable. Both hon. members were returned as representatives of the tenant farmers, and on the different questions which have arisen they have re- gularly taken the side of the tenants as against the landlords. The question of the appointment of the Committee on the Land Acts brought matters to a head. The Irish landlords in the house did their best to obstruct the Committee, and it was only un- der direct pressure from the Unionist leaders that they ultimately withdrew the block. As it is,there is no question that their objet will be to weaken rather than to facilitate the inquiry. •

The Women's National Liberal' Association, which I broke off two years ago from the Federation has arranged the 'programme of its conference, to be held in the following week, in a different manner The discussions are open to men and women; the question of women's suffrage is ignored; and the ' nebates, which are upon questioas of general poli- tics, are to be opened in each case by a member of Parliament whose name is closely associated with the subject. Mr. Johp.Dillon will introduce a dis- cussion upon "The rejection of the home Rule Bill," Mr. G. W. E. Russell on "Local Government," and Mr. Sidney C. Buxton, Mr. Thomas Burt, and Mr.S. Woods and Miss Gilliland will ,peak upon various Itspects of the labour question. Lady Hayter, Lady Fry,and Mrs. Bryce will preside over the successive

At the annual photographic conference of the Camera Club, which opened on Monday afternoon, Captain Hills, LE, set forth some of the more re- cent uses made of the camera for taking observa- tions as to latitude and lengitude and for other as- tronomical and surveying purposes. The accuracy of the methods by photography was declared to be ten times greater than with sextant observations. Mr. W. F. Yeames, R.A., in a paper on the relation of painting and photography, took the view that photography had led painters to pay greater atten- tion to the value of tones in light and shade and to the truthful rendering of accessories in pictures,but that it had also led them into realism and into giv- ing a preponderance of importance to values over c‘,lour, which was, aft.-.er all, that which gave the greatest life and poetical effect to a picture.

Among the delegat.-s from this country to the International Miners' Congress, to be held this year tit Berlin, will be Jr. Burt, M.P., Mr. Woods, M.P., Mr. Pickard, M.P., Mr. Fenwiek, M P., Mr. J. Wit- sort, M.P. (Durham), Mr. 11-liton, Mt. Cowley, Mr. Ofernall, and Mr. Edwards. Altogether the British delegates will number twenty or twenty-five.

I understand that he intends travelling to the nor- thern end of Lake Tanganyika, his main purpose being to collect specimens of the fauna and flora of the district for the British ..Nluseum, Air. Walter Itothehild's museum at Tting, Lord Wakingham's and other private collections. He will, however, also endeavour to do some good geogrdphical work by exploring the country to I.e mini' .i.vest of Tan- ganyika, and will, if posAhle, traverse the dark continent from east to west by completing his jour-. ney somewhere near the mouth of the Congo. llis collections will, however, be his chief aim, and his mements will be subordinated to them. Ilia absence may iange from two to five years,and there can be little doubt that when he returns our know- ledge of the zuology of the region traversed will be much advanced.

The Royal Horticultural eociety may be thoroQg hly congratulated on their show 011 Tuesday after- noon. Probably it was the very b -st, ot their smaller slow i seen at. West ininster. The National Aurieula and Primula Society was mkteh to the fore, ;mkt the chief prize they ga‘e was awarded to a really re- uietki.bly fine collection of auriculas. One herd:), knows how to foretell the future of these flowers, because they lend themselves so much to very sub- dued' and to very brilliant colours, the best of the latter being ‘iolet. Of course the &wizen—pale greens or greys with white centres—can only be appreciateu on a near view,but they are exceedingly lovely. There Were two curiosities in Tuesday's exhibition not so much admirable as noticeable. The roses were very lovely. and 1. tl,ink easily beat those exhibited at the Royal Botrinical Society's show recently held at Regeuee Park. I hear that the National Ant i-Oimbling League have taken what they call the "het advice " on , the question of racecourse etwtosures and turf clubs and that, as a result, they have determined to OW- lenge their legality. While the Lady Beatrice Hampton, in her char- acter of belle and leader of society, was thus gain- ing new commendations from the fashi6nable world, events were shaping themselves in such a way at The Evergreens as would wriug her heart with awful anguish. The evening f.,llowing her departure was passed by her secret family in the drawing- room, but a cloud seein-?,d to have faPen upon the young people, usually so merry and light-hearted. Accustomed as they had all their lives been to their mother's brief and stolen visits, they were too affectionate ever to have Leconte reconciled to them.

The count, his brows clouded with a gloom he could not shake off, and which he attributed to the tidings of Lord Adlowe's return, excused himself fin the little circle, that he might not be a shadow upon its gaiety, and went up to his study. The three children gathered together around the drawing-room Giralda's sweet ihoughtfulneso had deepened. Her thooghts seemed far away, and a strangely resolute (!preasion brooded in her dusky eyes. Pay leaned against her knee. Rupert sat beside her, his face bright and glowing with hie secret hopes mid aspirations. For some time not one of tho three spoke. At last little Fay broke the silence by a buret of • childish sobs.

" What is the matter, darling 1" asked his sister, alive at once to his grief. "I want mamma," cried the little fellow, with a grieved quiver of his lips, raising his wet violet eyes in a pleading expression that touched Giralda's heart. "Oh, I wish I were a man ! I would work and keep her always with us ! I miss her so when she has gone!" he added, with a moaning cry. "I know you do," said Giralda, soothingly, " but mamma would be distressed if she knew how you grieve every time she goes from us." Fay checked his sobs and tears, awl forced a pitiful little smile, more mournful than his tears had been. "Girabia," exclaimed Rupert, raising his spark- ling eyes to his sister's face, " I have the promise of goine into a university. You don't know how happy f am. I mean to be a great man yet, for mother's sake."

thri.l,la sympathised with his hopes as she had 'done with. 'y' grief. Yet every word spoken by tatter of the lads confirmed her secret resolution of lessening i;er mother's burden by enrnin,z, her own s.upport. Believing implicitly in the fiction of her ladyship's stage toils, she fancied herself bound in honour to care for herself.

The evening passed. The count returned to his family, and an hour of quiet, plem3ant conversa- tion followed. Then Me..,,,gy Fledt, the house- keeper, her as,irstant, and the coactiman, were called in to evening prayers, after which the little family separated for the night.

Left alone, Giralda ascended the stairs slowly, and went into her fathers study. Upon a table in u diSLant corner was a collection of itsily end weekly papers, Nviiio.l: the young people were rarely allowel to see, lest by some e:,unee they should come upon thei-e mother's natoe.

" Altlious:h f in tonic against the willot my dear parents, anti only frodi my love for them, and my desire to lessen their heavy burdens," she said, eoftly, "I must consider, above all, their wishes even in this matter. Mamma has never allowed one of her children to go to London. She would not app:eve my going there, even to help her. if I must go from home, she would like me to live in the family of some nice old gentleman. As a ' sseretaryandeempanion ' in Wales, I should lead a very secluded life. No one could or would harm me there. I feel &awn to answer this ad- vertisement. I cannot write to,Lord Trevelyan— the advertiser is is lord, it seems—because I cannot post or receive letters unnoticed. I must answer the advertisement in person. I must answer it to- morrow I" She leaned forward, staring earnestly at the fire. " I must thoroughly realize what I am about to do," she murmured. "1 am leaving a good home, the dearest and best of parents, the most loving of brothers—and for what? Papa writes books, but he dooe .not make enough from them to keep us in this style. My mother is an opera singer, or , actress, toiling for her children. I have been well educated. No money has been spared upon me. I can sing, dance, draw—can read and con- verse in several languages. Papa and mamma have aimed at making me a very accomplished woman. Ought I not to turn these accomplishments to use? I am past sixteen years of age. Is it not wrong for me to be a dead weight upon the dear hands of my mother 7', She considered the question long and earnestly. It was no girlish love of change that prompted I her desire to leave her home. She loved that home in which her sunny life had been passed. She worshipped her parents and brothers. She dreaded leaving those dear (men, and only her sense of duty upheld her in her purpose. "I must not be weak and childish,, ' she said, as tears dropped in quick succession from beneath her downcast eyelids. "I must think of the joy any parents will have when I send home my first earnings! lam the eldest child. I must help any mother." She knelt beside her chair in the warm glow of the firelight, and prayed for guidance, assistance and protection. Her upturned face was like the face of a young saint, in its rapt devotion and self- sacrifice.

She arose from her knees, her purpose strength- ened into a firm determination. - "Since I must apply at once," she said, "I must leave home this very night, in order to arrive at Trevelyan Ptrk in the morning." Her features were momentarily convulsed with pin, but she controlled her emotions by a resolute effort. She examined the contents of her purse, count- then with soft stem went to her

warctrove,!toset and brought fort 4 a Rust la leather travelling bag. Then she selected from th, drawers of her dre,,isi,:g bureau, under-linen, collars, and other neees6arios of the toilet. A &at ek bilk dress and a garnet merino filled the bag to a most pleth- oric; state.

Then she collected together all her girlish trin- kets, her petty necklaces and lockets, and rings, and shut them into her ebony jewel-cas- ket.

"I shall not Want those where I am going," she thought, hanging up the key. Her next movement was to dress for her jour- ney. She robed herself in a French gray cash- mere, with short skirt, just permitting to be visi- ble the tips of her dainty Polish boots, put on a fiannel-link:a jacket of the same material, a set of gray lamb-skin furs, and a jaunty hat of infini- tesimal proportions. "I don't look much like a nurse," she thought, dizappointedly, as she regarded the piquant re- flection in the glass. "But perhaps Lord Trevel- yan will think less of my looks, and more of my temper and willingness to please him." She pinned on her veil, and then brought her little malachite writing-desk to the fireside, and sat down to write a letter to her parents. It was a hard task. More than a dozen sheets of paper were destroyed and cast into the fire be- fore she succeeded in her effort. The letter, blotted with tears, was at length finished. It was addressed to her "dear parents." She told them simply that she deemed it her duty to work for her own support, and to assist them. She did not tell them where she was going. She gave them no clue to her intentions, beyond say ing that she should avoid London, and ever bear in mind th-e precepts she had been so carefully taught. She prayed them to forgive and bless her, and promised to write them again as soon u she could send them her first earnings.

That was all. But the traces of her bitter sor- row were visible in every line. She folded the letter, inclosed it in an envelope, and laid it on her unpressed pillow. Then, with choking sobs and tears, she looked around the pretty chamber for the last time. There were her treasured books and pictures, the statuettes she loved so well, all the little treasures of her childhood and young girlhood; the pretty bed where she had slept so peacefully, and dreamed such happy dreams; the great easy-chair where she had nestled • the bay window that had been to her a fairy palace. It was hard to leave them all—harder than she had thought. She bade a sileni; adieu to her room and its be- longings, calmed herself resolutely, and opened the door. She passed out into the hall, her trav- elling-bag in her hand, her veil pushed off from her 'face. She crept along silently to Rupert's door, paused, and listened. The sound of regular breathing came from within.

She softly opened the door and crept into the room.

Rupert was asleep. She could see his face, spirited even in repose, outlined against his pillow. She approached the bed and bent over her brother, dropping tears on his jetty hair. "Good-by, darling brother," she whispered, softly. "It is for you lam giving up what is most precious to me. Supply to our parents the place of Giralda, dear Rupert." She stooped and kissed him, the boy sleeping calmly on. A moment she stood there, then she turned and crept from the chamber as silently as she had en- tered it. _

She moved along the dim hall as silently as a spirit, and passed through into the room of little Fay. He was sleeping too. There was a night-lamp bnruing on the mantel-piece, and by its light the loving sister surveyed her darling—her mother's darling. How fair and sweet and gentle he was His cheeks just flushed with the tint of the wild-rose, his golden hair streaming over his pillow. Girahla's heart yearned over him. . .

She kissed him with passionate earnestness avain and again. When he stirred faintly, she hid behind the en:tains but came out again when he settled into deeper slumbers.

At last she tore herself away. She hurried along the hall to the aoor of her father's chamber. She knelt at his threshold and. breathed a silent prayer. Then she glided down the stairs, through the unlighted ',Aver hall, and hastened through the roams to a garden door. She unlocked it and passed out into the dark, windy night. She moved along the walk among the shadows, herself only a deeper shadow. 'When she had gained a little distance from the dwelling she paused and looked back. A light was gleaming from the windows of the ' count's study. She could just trace in the back- ground her father's face and figure as he sat at liis- -I..:riing table busy with his pen. wild impulse came over wraida to return. She struggled with it and resisted it, believing that the path she had entered upon was the path of (ilty. She excused her independence of her parents with the plea that her fond mother was sacrificing herself to keep her in idleness, and that when she bad shown that she was capable of supporting her- self both the couht and the Lady Beatrice would approve her course. - With an anguished farewell to her unconscious father, Giralda hurried to the garden gate. The key was hanging beside it. She unlocked it and passed out, latching it behind her. Then she flew along the dark country road towards the station, eager to catch the night train. • She arrived in time at the station, procured her ticktt, was seated in the train by the guard, and then went speeding on her way into the darkness and the night. She had 'fairly entered the great world ; had cast off the wise and loving guardianship of her parents through a strained sense of duty, and was moving boldly towards the snares from which her father and mother would have guarded her with their lives. She was going straight to her father's worst enemy—the vindictive and merciless Marquis Trovelyan To be continued.)

ANIMAL COMBATS AT THE ROMAN GAMES.

The first animal combat took place in the year s.e. 18. Bulls, bears, stags and many other beasts, exotic or borne-bred, fought together or with men, who were called " bestiares." A hundred years later rarer crea- tures were introduced, such as crocodiles, hippopotami, rhinoceroses, and even the giraffe. The appearance ot such animals says much for the cleverness of the hunters employed to cater for the Roman spectacles, since from gladiatorial days down to the early part of the preseno century no giraffes or hippopotami were brought to Europe owing to the extreme difficulty of capturing them alive.

At the fete of a hundred days given by Titus in the. rear 80, 5,000 savage beasts of various kinds were shown in one day, and at the spectacle given by Trajan, wliicn lasted four months, no less than 11,000 animal 3 were exhibited. Never was there such a splendid opportunity for the naturalist and the anatomist. The imperia. menageries were, as might be expected, extraordii.ariiy rich in rare creatures, and would quite have thrown our modern Zoological Gardens into the shade. The cost of the keep of so many carnivorous animals was enormous, and Caligula, in time of dearth, seriously considered the advisability of using criminals as fodder for his savage pets. . _

- The necessities of transport gave a wonderful stimu- lus to certain trades. The smiths and carpenters were kept hard at work making strong cages' while innumer- able traps and lassoes had to be provided for the hunters who were employed in all parts of the world to take ferocious beasts alive. In spite of anxious care and vast expense, many were the disappointments caused by the non-arrival of early expected living cargoes, owing to storms and contrary winds. During the long voyages and the interminable journeys overland, when the heavy cages were dragged by slow bullocks, many of the animals died, and others arrived in so pitiable a plight that they were quite incapable of " .showing light." .

The animals were usually introduced into the areni. ornamented with variegated scarves, metal plaques,. gold leaf, and tinsel. They were also painted in gaudy coleurs. Bulls were painted white, sheep purple, ostriches vermilion, and the lions had their manes gilded. The creatures were excited to fight by means of whips. and darts, and sometimes two of different species wer, tied together until, frantic with rage. they tore eacii other to pieces. Curious and unnatural contests were arranged, buffaloes being set to fight with bears, lions with giraffes, and tigers with rhinoceroses. Chassems, with English or Scotch hounds, fought the most savage beasts, and a distinguished champion could kill a bear with a blow of his fist, or vanquish a lion after blinding him by throwing a cloth over his eyes. The Roman animal trainers must have been men of extraordinary genius, and would certainly have put our modern trainers to the blush. We hear of Julius Ctesar being lighted to his house by elephants carrying torches in their trunks, and Mark Ant ony being drawn through the streets by lions harnessed to his chariot. Unwieldv bulls learnt to walk on their hind legs, stags were trained to obey the bit, and panthers to bear the yoke.. Peaceable antelopes were taught to fight with their horns, and fierce lions were rendered as docile as dogs. In one of the spectacles given by Dornitian a perfortnieg lion carried hares into the arena in his mouth withein- hurting them, let them go, and caught them spin. Elephants wrote Latin verses and danced on the tight rope. Pliny tells el one of these animals who learnt lees quickly ',llan its fellows,and being anxious,presumablyoss catch them up, or to escape punishment, was discovered rehearsing its lesson in the middle of the night. Far more horrible and demoralising than the cemhsts were the wholesale executions of prisoners, who were bound to pests in the middle of the arena, and devoured by the beasts that were let loose upon. Semetmes the, poor creatures were provided with arms, which only served to prolong their agonies for a few moments. In the year 47 multitudes of Breton prisoners were masse- cred in this fashion at Rome, while at the conclusiee ef the Jewish war 2,500 Jewish prisoners perished at Cetsarea during the public games. Pantomimes and tableaux vivants were terribly realistic entertainments under the Empire. Criminals appeared dressed in magni- ficent garments, from which filmes sodden': butes: forth and consumed the wearers. lxion was shown en wheel, and Mucius Scaevola holding his hand in ;A brazier until it was reduced to nshes. Another poet- wretch was supposed to represent Orpheus. He plase•i upon the lute, and all nature seemed subjegsted be 1): performance. Trees bowed down to listen to him, timid animals followed him, and birds perched upon his head. Just as the spectatores were beginning to weary of this innocent amusement a heir rushed out, and beingpresumably insensible to the charms of music, devoured the unfortunate minstrel amid general applause.

SOMETHING LIKE A PARK!

The Yellowstone National Park (U.S.A.) is a magnifi- cent domain in its proportions, extending nearly six,y- five miles from north to south.and fifty-five miles from east to west. The Yellowstone Lake lies near tha south-eastern corner of the district, the Yellowstone River flowing from its upper boundarv, and runnirez almost due north. The lake is twenty-tw, miles in length, its average width from ten to fifteen miles. Is height above the level of the sea is sgven thousand feet, while its basin is surrounded by mountains reaching an altitude of over ten thousand feet, the peaks of which are covered with perpetual snow. Numerous hot springs are found on the shores of lake, and also along the banks of the river. About fifteen miles from its source,the river takes two distinct. precipitous leaps, known as the Upper and the Lower Falls, and beyond the fells cuts its way through an im- mense canon,the vertical walls of which reach at placos. the height ef fifteen hundred feet. Near the western boundary of the park the Madison, an important tribu- tary of the Columbia, takes its rise ; and along on ef the branches of this river, known as Fire-Hole River, are found numerous extraordinary geysers, some or which throw volumes ef boilinc, water to a !eight en.- needing two hundred feet. In the north•western cot ner of the park the Gallatin, another tributary of the Columbia, takes its rise.

HELP IN TIME OF NEED.

What a crust of bread is to the starving, Warner's Safe 'Care is to the afflicted. It is a true friend when all else fails, in diseases of the kidneys, liver, and urinary organs, appearing as the saving angel. It stands alone as the remedy for these diseases, and is acknowledgedas such by medical authorities of all countries. It is, therefore, logical that persons afflicted with any of the above diseases or complaints should try this or that experiment, grovelling in the dark, only to result in repeated disappointment, in place of promptly resorting to Warner's Safe Cure, the proper and only remedy. The following marvellous cure is only one of hundreds of thousands that have been effected by this remedy : " About four years ago," writes Mr. Jas. A. Joy, 68, Corporation-street, Manchester, under date of April 18th, 1894, " I had an attack of Influenza, which kept me in bed four weeks. When I riot up and about again I was very nervous ; could get no sleep, could not eat, and *offered with pains in the head and face which almost drove me mad. I got worse and worse, week after ~week. Of course I had medical advice, and various 'clads of medicine, from none of which I received any benefit. When things got to the worst, and everybody, including myself, had given up all hope, I met a friend Whom I had not seen for some time, and seeing the con- dition I was in (I had lost 4 stone in weight), he said, is Try Warner's Safe Cure." I laughed at the idea. No quack medicine for me, thank you.' But he insisted, Yhag Don't get one bottle and use it, and then say it uo good. Your case is too serious for that. It has taken years to brio.- you to your present condition, and You won't be cured in a week, but if you will get bottle after bottle for a month, and take same as per instruc- tions, and you do not find yourself well on the road ta recovery at the end of that time, I will pay you for all You have taken, and stand a guinea silk hat into the bargain.' I yielded and got a large bottle, and religi- °Wily took eight di ses a day. When that was done I Xot another, and followed suit. At the end of two weeks I felt no better, though every one else congratulated me Upon the improvement that had taken place. This en- couraged me to go on, which I did for about lour months. At the end ot that time I was as well as ever I had been iU my life, gaining weight all the time. Since then I have never had a day's illness, but I am never without &Small bottle of Warner's Safe Cure in the house. ANNUAL MEETING OF WYCLIFFE P.S A. On Monday evening the first annual meeting of the above named society for the consideration of the reports and balance sheet and the election of officers was held in 'Wycliffe Hall. The Rev. J. Tonge presided. Mr. T. WALLINGTON secretary, submitted his first annual report. He said he thought as members and officials they all could look back with thankfulness on the year's work just completed, and he was of the °Pinion that the inoyement had been one ot the greatest successes in religious work that had been taken up in the town, a success not simply from the actual numbers , attending the meetings, but the effect it had had and Was having on the lives and characters of the men. Referring to the attendances at the 52 meetings held up to February 25, the average attendance for the first half Year was 500, and for the second half year 800, or an average for the year of 650. 'those figures were only aPproximate and if anything were rather under the actual attendance. The secretary then dealt with the membership and number of prizes and certificates dis- tributed. In conclusion, he said they had every encour- agement to go forward in their work. If only they went forward in the same spirit that they had done in the year Which had just closed, the Wycliffe P.S.A. would bo a 'flighty instrument for good in Warrington. (Ap- Plause.) Mr. R. WYCLIFFE P.S.A. SOCIETY.

The meeting of this society on Sunday was presided over by Mr. D. R. Mason, who spoke on the "power and influence of gentleness." He stated that all the forces of nature were gentle in their action. Tney were not sur- prised at that because the great Creator himself was gentle in his dealings with his creatures. He advised his hearers to take this gentleness to their workshop, office, and home, and they would he surprised at the result. Mr. W. Hickman delivered the address and took for his subject "A leaf" drawing some useful lessons from it. Mrs. Gleave was the soloist and Mr. W. H. Payton also contributed an organ solo. Messrs. J. Irish and F. Knowles alto took part in the service.

'BAPTIST ENDEAVOUR SOCIETY.-ANNIVERSARY

MEETING.

About one hundred persons assembled in the Baptist Sunday School, Golborne-st , on Tuesday eveninE.,:, when the first anniversary meeting of the Baptist Younv People's Society of Christian Endeavour No. 638) was held. The room had been quietly but efTectivelY deco- rated for the occasion, and it presented a cosy appear- ance.

In the absence of the president, Mr. H. Beckby tvice- president) occupied the chair, and,after devetional exer- cises, addressed the assembly. Ile said that two years ago the young people connected with the church thought that some effort should be made to bring them together during the week. It happened that, at that time, the church was without a pastor, and little interest seemed to be taken in anythirig, save the Sunday services. This state of things did not suit the young people, and they made their desires known to several members who took an interest in them. The result was the formation ef a Christian Guild, which was successfully carriee on dur- ing the winter months. The lectures, entertainments, and Bible classes were all that could be desired, but the young people felt that even these did not meet their spiritual aspirations. Consequeetly it was decided, a year ago, to start a Christian Endeavour, and they wore met that night to celebrate their first anniversary. In the year through which they had passed, the C. E. had been of the greatest benefit to the members. It had kept young people off the streets, it had given them opportu- nities for Christian work, and it had helped them in the study of the Bible. The C. E. had been of further bene- fit in helping the members to speak in public,and,in this respect, the lady members had gained as much as the gentlemen. The past year's work and experience had been of inestimable advantage to them, and to none more than to those who, through the society's agency, had enlisted under Christ's banner; and had consecrated their lives to His service. The meetings increased in interest, and he trusted their last gathering, which was the best they had held,was a foretaste of the good times that awaited them in the coming year. Miss Rose Ward, the recording secretary, then read the annual report. She said that the society started with 15 active, and four associate members, but these members soon increased to 37 active and 13 asso- ciate numbers. Several defections had, however, occurred from various causes, and the roll now stood as follows:-4 honorary, 30 active, and 9 ass eciate mem- bers. During the year two active members had become honorary members,eight associates had joined the active ranks, and 10 members had joined the church. The society had displayed considerable missionary zeal, and their labours had resulted in the formation of threeother societies—one in Widnes, and two in Warrington. The society had also orgareised cottage prayer meetings, which had been productive of great good. and it was hoped that the year's labours would stimulate the mem- bers to do more "for Christ and the Church" in the coming year than they had done in the past, Mr. John S. Green (treasurer) read the financial state- ment,from which it appeared that the members monthly contributions had amounted to £1 10s. 3d., the total income being £2 ss. 9d. The expenditure, which had been mostly incidental to the formation of the society, amounted to £ll7s. mid., and this sum incloeeci special donation to the Thornhill Colliery Disaster Fund. There was however, an eutstandine acceunt, whieh left the society with a debt of Bs. 7j ;bbut it was hoped, by the liberality of that assembly, to liquidate that amount and thus be able to have a clear start for the new year. It should be mentioned that the society was instru- mental in raising a handsome sum towards the local distress fund during the colliers strike, besides contri- buting to the assistance of several of its members who were affected by the coal dispute. Miss Mary Few read the paper for the evening, the topic being " God's care for His Own." The interest of the gathering centred in the c ensecra- don meetieg, when responses were given to the query —" What has the Christian Endeavour done for me ?" Every active member testified to the help the Society had been to him or her, and the replies may be sum- marised thus—The C.E. had given the members oppor- tunities of service, it had helped them to witness for • Christ, it had increased their interest in the scriptures, it had helped them in their daily life, it had won them for the church, and it had drawn them nearer to Christ

At the conclusion of the meeting,which was of a stimu- lating character, refreshments were served by the social Committee, and a social hour was spent.

INDUCTION OF CHURCHWARDENS AT WARRINGTON.

The annual induction of churchwardens and sidesmen of the Warrington district of the diocese of Liverpoe,l was held at the Warrington Parish Church on Wednes- day morning, the Rev. Dr. Taylor, archdeacon of War- rington, officiating. After prayers had been conductf'd by the Rector of Warrington (Rev.l4'. W. Willis), the Rev. Dr. Taylor in the course of his charge to the wardens and sidesmen, said the duty of the churchwardens was to look after the fabric and ministrations of the church ,the sitting arrangements, and the churchyard. They were the guardians of all that belonged to the church, and of the interests of the parishioners whom they represented. They were to bee that all the services were duly per- formed, and that the church was provided with the things essential for that purpose, and that it was kept in proper repair. They should be regular in their atten- dance, and should alsw encourage others to do the same, both by their example and by friendly persuasion. They must be ready to assist the minister as might be neces- sary, and see that the people were comfortably seated. They had no power to interfere with the minister in the performance of the service whatever. With reference to the expense necessarily involved in carrying on the services of the church, he might say that they had of late years increased very much. In former wears the church expenses were very small,and £2O to £4O a year would have been sufficient, and could have been pro- vided out of the church rates. Now the expenses were increased fourfold, and the church rates were abolished. Hence the expenses had to be provided out of the weekly offertories, collections, and subscriptions. There was a difference between the offertories 'and the collections which was not always realised. The offertory proper was when the alms of the people were collected durin!, the reading of the offertory sentences appointed to be read. Collections as distinguished from the offertory were the moneys collected at other times. The disposal of the former rested with the minister and, the churigh wardens working together, and were to be used for various charitable purposes, and educational purposes. The disposal of the other collections rested with the minister and him alone. Still, it was always better for the minister and the churchwardens to work together in all cases. He did not think that for the parishioners to attend the church only in the evening was sufficient,and he urged upon the wardens and sidesmen to use their influence to promote better attendance. The attendance was not nearly so good as it was 20 years ago. He hoped the ancient custom of Easter offerings would be restored whenever desirable, as they offered a simple and inex- pensive made of substantially increasing the minister's stipend. He was also pleased to see the success of the clergy sustentation fund, as it had materially assisted many clergymen. He thought that the weekly offertory and collections ought to be much larger, as it would not make much difference to the people if they increased their offerings from a halfpenny to a penny or twopence,while it would make a great difference to the collection. In corn- ing to the qualifications of churchwardens, he said that as the present was an age of liberty, people went to the church they liked the best,and did not attend the church in the parish they resided. In these cases he thought it was desirable that the management of the church should be left as much as possible to those who attended it,, nd provided the funds. Of course, Nonconformists, Roman Catholics, and Jews could attend the vestry meetings, but still lie did not think they did attend as a rule. As to the notice convening the vestry, as far as he was ad- vised, it was only necessary that it should be affixed to the principal door of the church, and three clear days must elapse, exclusive of the day of notice and the (lily of meeting. It was not necessary, as some people sup- posed,that the notice should be given in the church. At the close of the Archdeacon's charge, the wardens and sidesmen signed the declaration upon taking their office, and the proceedings then terminated.

Mr. A. N. Hornby has declined the Unionist invitation to become a candidate for the Crewe division. The Queen has approved the appointment of Sir Chas. Russell as a Lord of Appeal in Ordinary, in room of the late Lord Bowen. Mr. T. W. Russell, M.P., informed a press representa- tive on Tuesday that ho had definitely decided not to seek re-election to the House of Commons. In giving his reason for withdrawing from the South Tyrone the hon. gentleman said "It is because I cannot help myself." The Judge of the City of London Court has ordered H. G. WRIGHT, formally solicitor to the Liberator Build- ing Society, to refund several thousand pounds received by him as "secret profits "on transactions which he con- ducted on behalf of the Society. WRIGHT is undergoing penal servitude for participation in the Liberator frauds.

NEW SCHOOLS FOR HAYDOCK.

Tha foundation stone of new township Fchools at Blacl(hrook, near Sr. Helens, was laid on Saturday afternoon by Mr. T. W. Legh, M.P., in the presence of a large concourse of people. Amongst those present were the Revs. C. E. Bowden, vicar of Sr. James's, Flaydock, J. Andrews, T. R. Matthews, Earlestown, M. F. Binney. Sutt)n. Col. R. Pilking- ton, Mr. E. J. George, Mr. John Jar_dison, and Dr. Hay- ward. The want of school accommodation has been long felt in the district, and the Education Department having signified that additional Accommodation must be provided or a school board established, it was decided to ascertain the opinions of the inhabitants on the subject. As the result of several meetings, it waS agreed that a voluntary rate of one shilling in the pound should bk‘ laid, a,vil that schools be built from the sum realised. The ‘Yoluntary rate on the assessment would realise £1,055, and this amount being assured, the contract for the building of the schools was let to Mr. Tickle, of Sutton. They are intendA to accommodate 350 children and to cost about £1,400.

The ceremony of laying the stone was performed by Mr. T. W. Leg,h, M.P.. who was called upon by the Rev. C. E. Bowdeh. _ _ .

_ Tee stone having been laid, Mr. Legh said he need scarcely enure them that it was a great pleasure that he found himself there that day taking part in that ceremony, which marked a courageous effort on the pert, of the people of that diArie.t to educate their children in the tenets they considered best for their future moral welfare. He was not there to defend the voluntary sys- tem ;it did not require any defence At his hands. It had been tried long ago, and had not been found want- ing, but ha would remind them that when more the:.! twenty years ago the late Mr. Forster introduced his education act, he expressed a confident Opinion that no man would he found in the future who would seek to do harm to the voluntary system and voluntary teach- ing in this country. He grieved to say that the support- ers of the system had fallen upon evil days. For the last year or two the voluntary system had been atta.cked, in a harrassing and, he might almost say malicious man- ner, and they had all the more reason to complain of the unreasonable demands made upon them. It was evident to his mind that there were people who unfortunately were now responsible for the educational policy of the country, who were not indisposed to injure the volen- tary system, and he would ask them would the system by which it is proposed to replace it conduce to their ad- vantage in the educational point of view ? He said it. would not. If the nation decided to abolish the volun- tary system, it would be necessary to provide places for 3,659,000 children,becausft he took it that the voluntary schools could not be confiscated without compensation. If they abolished the voluntary system and established board schools, they .would have to find £40,000,000,and furnish annually a,pout £2,000,000. Those whom he was addressing were, he presumed, in favour of the vol- untary system on account of religious teachlng, and he asked them was it worth while to make the change, as he maintained they would gain nothing by it. Ile con- cluded by appealing to them to assist all in their power to make the schools a success. (Applause.) On the motion of the Rev. J. Andrews,a vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Lech, which that gentleman acknow- ledged, and the gathering dispersed.

CATHOLIC BAZAAR AT WIDNES.

OPENING CEREMONY BY THE LORD MAYOR OF LIVERPOOL.

On Wednesday afternoon, the Lord Mayor of Liverpool (Alderman Bowring)oPened.a four days' bazaar in the Drill Hall, Widnes in connection with St. Bede's Catholic Church, Appleton, Widnes. The object of the bazaar is to raise funds in aid of the cost of the new presbytery erected last year. It may be stated that St. Bede's is one of the oldest Catholic churches in the Liverpeol district. The present rector (the Rev. Thomas Finegan) succeeded Father Fisher in 1875, and received the tide of dean in 1886. During his pastorate the church has been decorated twice, a new bell purchased, the organ completed, the girls' and infants' school enlarged, a reredos to the centre altar erected, and commodious boys' schools built. It has been through his energetic lab ;urs that the mission has just been provided with the new and handsome presby- tery. The bazaar represents the town of St. Milo, on the coast of Normandy, during the eighteenth century. The seven stalls are arranged to represent the street leading te the market-place, the ancient houses being utilised for the display of the laree variety of goods offered for sale. It is thoueht to be the most picturesque bazsar yet seen in Widnes. There was a large attendance at the opening ceremony, which was presided over by the Rev. Dean Finegan. Among those present on the platform were the Lord May or of Liverpool (Alderman Bowfing, who was accompanied by the Lady Mayoress, Mr. H. Wade Deacon, C.C. Mr. J. Morrison (chairman of the committee), Mr. F. Eleyes (treasurer), Messrs. C. Finn and C. Broom (hon. secretaries), several members of the Town Council, and a large number of the Catholic clergy of the neighbourhood.—The Rev. Dean Finegan, in in. troducing the Lord Mayor, said not only St. Bede, but the whole town of Widnes were honoured by the pre- sence of the Lord Mayor of Liverpool and the Lady Mayoress, and it was an honour which they would long remember.—The Lord Mayor, who was enthusiastically received, said he felt it his duty and pleasure to further anything which he believed was fur the moral, eocial, and commercial welfare of the community in that neigh. bourhood. (Applause.) They could flat all agree in ecclesiastical matters. There were differences of dogma, but he was glad that there was one thing in which they could all unite, and that was inculcating, advancing', and enforcing the moral law laid down by Jesus ChriA. His lordship then formally declared the lazier open.— Mr. J. Morrison proposed a vote of thanks to the Lord Mayor and Lady Mayoress for their presence, which was he said, an honour to the town of Widnes.—Councillor H. B. Shores seconded. The Lord Mayor, he said, was notable for the breadth of his philanthropy and the catholicity of his aims.—The motion was carried with acclamation, and the Lord Meyor suitably responded, which concludf d the proceedings.

A STRANGE CASE.

John Stocks, hair-dresser, of the Market-place, Warrington, was on Saturday, found in Priestley- street suffering from a slight wound in the throat. He was taken to the Police Station, and on being examined was found to be of unsound mind, and unable to give a satisfactory account as to how he received the wound. Subsequently he was taken by a relieving officer to the hospital at the work- house.

P.S.A. AIEETING.

Mr. W. H. Bayley, presided at the P.S.A. meeting at the Young Men's Christian Associa- tion on Sunday. He spoke upon the motto adopted by the society for 1804, viz., "Help one another." Mr. Kent (president) gave the address, and chose as his subject " Father Damien, his life and labour among the Lepers of Molokai." The address was listened to with marked attention. Mr. Parkinson was the soloist, and rendered two solos, from the "Daughter of Jairus," and from " Saul " respectively in his' usual able manner. Mr. Pict on accompanied. GOLF BALL.—GARLAND Ironmonger, Warrington and Earlestown.

SUDDEN DEATH.

On Sunday morning, Hannah Edwards, 35 years of age, wife of John Edwards, engine driver, resid- ing at 38, Priestley-street, was found dead in bed at about eight o'clock. For about three years, the deceased had suffered from a tumour in the brain, and had been to Manchester for treatment at the eye hospital, about six weeks ago. She had been blind for about 18 months and recently complained of a severe pain in her.head. On Saturday night, she ntired to rest about ten o'clock, and when her husband awoke the next morning, he found her dead in bed. The 19th annual meeting of the Cheshire Lines Platelayers and Workmen's Provident Society, which consists of the workmen employed in the Engineer's Department of the Cheshire Lines Rail- way, was held at the Works, Bat tersby Lane, War- rington, on Tuesday. In the unavoidable absence of the engineer, Mr. W. G. Scott, M. Inst. C. E., the chair was taken by Mr. W. Corker, permanent way Inspector, and the meeting was largely attended by officials and delegates from Lancashire and Cheshire. The Secretary submitted the annual report, which stated that the number of members was 986. A large sum had been expended in aid of 232 sick members, who had received assistance from the funds of the Society, and 18 death claims had also been paid. The report and statement of accounts having beeta passed, Mr. Thomas Pratt was re-elected Secretary for the ensuing year, and Messrs. G. Walker and W, Hughes were re-elected auditors. The proceeslings terminated with a vote of thanks to the chairman, offitl-trs and others. PETROLEUM OILS,—GARLAND, 51, Bridge-street, Warring- ton.

NARROW ESCAPES FROM DROWNING: RESCUED

BY A LIEEBOAT.

For some considerable time a number of pleasure boats have been stationed on the river near War- rington Bridge for the purpose of hire. But owing to the little business done, the proprietor found it necessary to remove his stand, and decided to pro- ceed to Chester with them. Accordingly the boats numbering seven, were taken up the river on Sun- day night, and the men in charge of them were Aaron Whitby, Thomas Brooks, William Hopkins, Samuel Tilling, and Edward Walker. Four of the men belong. to Warrington. On Monday morning the Point of Ayr lifeboat, in charge of Coxswain S. Evans, on her passage to New Brighton, to take part in Monday's demonstration in aid of the National Lifeboat Institution, saw the seven in dis- tress in Iloylake Bay. She bore down upon them, and found that the frail vessels, owing to the strong tide and choppy sea, were fast filling. Five men were in charge of the boats which were at once taken in tow and placed in a position of safety. It is probable that the five men would have lost their lives had it not been for the timely assistance ren- dered by the Point of Ayr lifeboat.

ALL HOUSETIOLD LINEN, hemmed and marked. free of charge.—J. and W. DuTros. Sankey-et. Warrington,—Advt. ANOTHER CLAY PIT ACCIDENT.—RESCUE 37

FATHER. An exciting scene happened behind Welfield-st., on Saturday evening. A number of children were playing about a clay pit, when one of their number was seen to fall into the water. An alarm was immediately raised by the other children. The child's father, Mr. llought.on, who lived hard by, rushed to the pit on hearing the alarm, and though he cannot swim, he dashed into the water, which is about seven feet. deep, and even- tually succeeded in reachinw the hank with the child. The latter, however, was quite unconscious and apparently lifeless. At the same time Mr. Wm. •-mith, a Sunday school teacher at St. Paul's Church, came upon the scene, and immediately set about resuscitating the Child. For some time his effoits appeared to be futile, but alter renewed attempts he induced the water to rush from the child's mouth, ears, and nose, which had the effect of bringing it to coniciousness. The child was then conveyed home, simultaneously with the arrival of Dr. Braide, who successfully applied restoratives. The unfortunate child, who is only two and a half years old, remained for some time in a very precarious state. UMBRELLAS BE-COVERED, —J. and W. DUTTON, Sankey-it., Warrington.—Advt.

THE Rim C. E. JACKSON'S FAREWELL. The Rev. Charles E. Jackson, late senior curate of the Parish Church, and who had been curate-in- charge of St. George's Mission since its opening, preached his farewell sermon in that church on Sunday evening, prior to his departure from War- rington to take up his new duties as Rector of Nant- wich.—On Monday evening a farewell tea was held in the St. George's Mission Room, when several presentations were made by the congregation and others to the Rev. C. E. Jackson. A large number sat down to *,ea, after which a capital programme of music was gone through. The Rector (the Rev. F. W. Willis), who made the pret :ntations, said that before he did so be would like o say a few words to those present. He took up is duties as rector of Warrington in 1888. Mr. Jackson came shortly before he did, and during the six years he had been there they owed much to him for the many wise suggestions he had put forward. Mr. Jackson had been principally connected with Crossley-st. and St. George's. Three years he was at Crossley-st. mission and they were all aware of the difficulties under ' which he laboured there. However three years ago the foundation sone of St. George's was laid by Lady Walker, and principally through the exertions of Mr. Jackson and Mr. J. E. Wright, those beautiful buildings were erected. During the two and a half years they had had the use of them, various branches of work had been carried on. Mr. Jackson had carried on the Sunday school work, and it had pro- gressed in a most satisfactory manner. There were 400 scholars, and the school generally was in excel- lent condition. In fact, all the branches mentioned had been started and carried on by Mr. Jackson, who possessed great energy and ability, He hacf now a great work before him, a very important sphere indeed, and they could show their apurecia- don of his services by praying for his future sue- ces. On their behalf he made the following . presentations —A carved oak chair, from the congregation, workers, and others; a settee from the Sunday school teachers and scholars, and Bible class, and mothers' meeting; a framed photograph, from the Sunday school teachers; framed photo- graphs, from the club and members of working- men's club. (Applause.)—Messrs. Banner and Ward , baring made a few remarks, the, 'Rev, C. E. Jackson rveponded in suitable terms.

DEATH OF MR. JOSEPH B. SENIOR.

On Sunday aftertiOO-ti -t-li;;;---wei:e—ia;r-re'd at the Warrington Cemetery the remains of the late Mr. Joseph B. Senior, of Howard-street. The deceased died on the previous Thursday. He had been in failing health fora long time. He was 71 years of age, and was 35 years ago a familiar figure in Wesleyan circles in Warrington. Ile always took an active and devoted interest in matters affecting the Church, especially in connection with the Butter- market-street Sunday School, at that time the school belonging to Bold-street Chapel, where for many years he held the office of teacher, and after- wards was elected superintendent. On his leaving Warrington in 1863, he was the recipient of a hand- some family Bible inscribed with expressions of the highest regard by the teachers and officers of the school. Prior to this he was employed at the brass wire and pin manufactory of Messrs. J. B. Edelsten and Co., where, by his integrity and courteous bear- ing, he won the esteem of his employers and fellow- workmen alike. Circumstances led him at this time to remove to North Woolwich, in order to take a re- sponsible position in the making of the Atlantic telegraph cable. He was entrusted with the im- portant duty of superintending the paying out of the cable from the works to the Great Eastern. About four years ago the deceased returned to War- rington, and became attached to the Bewsey-road Church, where his worth was quickly recognised, land he came to be regarded as a most valued mem- ! ber. His somewhat feeble health prevented him from taking that active part in church work that, was formerly his wont. Many signs of esteem and affection attended the interment on Sunday. The funeral service was conducted by the Rev. John ' Gunnell, and was largely attenied by representa- tive officials and leaders from the Bewsey-road and Bold-street Societies. Amongst those present were Messrs. John Toft, Edwin Richardson, F. Dixon, S. G. Davey, W. Winstanley, A. H. Dunn, J. W. Hay- ward, J.P., Isaac Hayward, G. Gough, George Bay- ley, Isaac Johnson (leader of the deceased's society class) and many of the members. The service was brought. to a close by singing one of the deceased gentleman's favourite hymns.

ALL HOUSEHOLD LINEN, hemmed and marked, free of charge.—J. and W. DUTTON, •Sankey-st. Warrington,—Advt. FATAL ACCIDENT AT THE L. AND N.- W. GOODS WAREHOUSE.

Sir. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner, held an inquest at the Patten Arms, on Monday afternoon, upon the body of John Bancroft, a goods porter for the L. and N.-W. Railway, who was killed, by being crushed by a waggon on Saturday afternoon, at the goods warehouse, Bank Quay station. Mr. Fenna appeared on behalf of the L. and N.-W. Railway.—Margaret Bancroft said that deceased was her husband and resided at Dunham Massey. He was a goods porter ;at Bank Quay station. He left home on Saturday morning about eight o'clock. She heard of her ' husband's death on Saturday afternoon. He was 38 years of age.—John Jones, porter, said that he was working on the same " turn" as deceased at No. 2 North side. Deceased was working at 2.45 on Saturday, engaged in transferring the goods from one van to another. It was not his duty to uncouple a waggon. His duty was simply to remove goods from one van to another. He was in the warehouse in the " six feet," the iast time wit- ness saw him. He was then about 14 feet from where he was killed. Witness did not know what he was doing then. Soon afterwards, he heard a shout from deceased, and on going to see what was the matter, he found deceased lying in the " six ' feet." When asked where lie was hurt he replied that he was hurt in his stomach. He only lived a quarter of an hour afterwards.—Cross-examined by Mr. Fenna : The accident was caused by a waggon coming into the warehouse. Witness saw it coming at a walking rate and lie shouted out to toe others in the van "Look out, a van is coming." A man named William Weave also shouted out. He did not see the deceased at toe Weave, employed in the goods department,said that he saw the waggon coming into the warehouse. He had not seen the deceased for five minutes previously and that time he was on the stage at the warehouse. It was not part of the deceased's duties to uncouple the waggons and no waggons needed uncoupling at the time. There were two waggons coupled before the accident, and after the accident they were uncoupled. Witness thought deceased had uncou- pled the waggons to get through more easily. Deceased had been in the service for about three years and had always been a steady rnan.—The jury returned a verdict of" Accidental Death." Mr. Fenna said that the deceased was insured for £BO, which would be given without any inquiry into any contributory negligence.

LYMM.

A GIPSY'S WARNINGS. —WHAT THEY COST.—At the Lymm Police Court, on Tuesday morning, before Mr. T. P. Norris and Mr. A. Watkins, Dinah Boswell, of Bale Moss, Altrinchatn, was charged with having, between the months of October, 1893, and April 1894, obtained certain sums of money, altogether amounting to 31s. from a domestic servant named Miriam Miller, of Lymm, on the pre- tence of telling her fortuae.—Mr. Paterson, of Bowden, represented the prisoner.—From the evidence it ap- peared that prisoner called upon the girl in October last, and told her something terrible was going to happen, and that she was in a position to prevent her losing her situation, and her" young man" if she would give her (prisoner) some money. The girl thereupon gave her a sovereign. Prisoner produced a pack of cat ds,and hav- ing asked for a red silk handkerchief,and this having been handed to her, she wrapped the sovereign in it, and crossed the cards with it. Sne then gave the girl a picce of paper and told her to put it in her breast and not let anyone know about it. She called again in November and informed the girl that she had discovered that the secret of the paper had been divulged, and theretere she could not go on with the charm unless the girl gave her Bs. 6d. The money was given to her, and in February she called again and obtained 2s. 61. under similar cir- cumstances. She again appeared before the girl on Monday, when Sergeant Garner appeared on the scene and took her into custody. On the way to the police station she dropped the cards in the road.—The magis- trates inflicted a fine of £3 and costs, or in default one month's imprisonment with hard labour. The money was paid.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMEN'Is.

FRIDAY.

In the House of Commons on Friday Mr. A. C. MORTON moved a resolution declaring that, the Duke of Edinburgh having succeeded to the sovereignty of a foreign Country, it was desirable that the annuity granted him by the British parliament should cease._ Mr. Labouchere seconded the motion. Dr. HUNTER said the question was whether the House was going to violate a contract. After a singular speech by Lord R. Churchill, delivered amid shouts of laughter and much interruption, Sir W. HARCOURT said the question the Government had to determine was whether it was a , proper act for them to revoke or reduce the grant, and they came to the conclusion that it was not proper to reduce it. He trusted that the matter would not be taken to a division. Mr. BALFOUR also spoke against the motion,and said he had always felt that, as regarded . the relationship which ought to subsist between Parlia- ment and the reigning family, Mr. Gladstone was a guide whom they might all be proud to follow. On a division Mr. Morton's motion was defeated by 298 te 67

===== 0002601_18940428_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
MONDAY.

The House of Commons went into Committee of Ways and Means on Monday evening, and Sir Wm. HARCOURT moved the Budget resolutions dealing with the succes- sion duties and the income tax. He said that by the plan of the Government the total yield on death duties would be 131. millions (as against 10 million:: at present), and of that personalty would pay 11 millions and realty 211 millions. The additional charge on realty would he £1,300,000, of which less than half would fall upon agricultural land. But there was an allowance under schedule A of several thousand pounds, and really £350,000 was asked of the landed interest as their con- ' tribution to the taxation for the defence of the country and to place taxation on an equitable basis. After a short discussion it was agreed tt defer the resolution on the estate duty. Mr. BALFOUR charged the Chancellor of the Exchequer with having provided every sixpence to make up his deficit at the expense of the wealthy classes, in violation of the principles laid down in the Budget. The income tax resolution was eventually agreed to. I Debate on the motion for the second reading of the Con- ciliation (trade Disputes) Bill was resumed a few minu- tes before midnight, but Mr. Pierpoint again opposed, and the debate had to be adjourned. The Solicitors Examination Bill was read a third time.

In the House of Lord 4 the Marquis of LONDONDERRY. after interrogating the Earl of Cork respecting the mur- der on the noble Earl's estate, said that on Friday he should ask the Government. whether they attributed the crime to the rash speeches of their supporters, and whether they would now reconsider their policy with regard to the repeal of the Crimes Act. The Select Com- mittee on the subject of betterment was appointed.

===== 0002601_18940428_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY.

In the House of CommQns on Tuesday evening Mr. A. MORLEY informed Mr. Schvyann that without statutory. authority he had no power to stop the circulation of foreign lottery advertisements through the Post-office. 119 added that he would consider the question and see if anythinr, could be done. In reply to questions, Sir W HARCOURT said he thought it was not probable that the Local Veto Bill would he in- troduced before Whitsuntide. Ho hoped it would- be introduced afterwards.

The right lion. gentleman told Colonel Nolan that he could not undertake at present to premise an opport- unity to get through Committee the bill for the repeal of the Crimes Act.

Discussion of the Budget provsals was resumed in Committee of Ways and Means. On the resolution deal- ing with the graduated estates duties, Mr. CHAPLIN said this was not the time to increase the burden of taxation on the land. Sir C. DILKE expressed approval of the Government proposal. Mr.. FOWLER said that at no time for which statistics were available during the present century had the average rural rates been so low and the London rates so high as in 1891. Sir M. HICKS-BEACH said graduated death duties would be a very great ex- periment, and he believed that in the case of personalty they would be largely evaded. Sir. W. HARCOURT in defending his scheme, said he felt confident that when- ever the principle of a larger contribution of the rich towards the burdens of the State was put before them the majority of the people of the country would be strongly in favour of it. The resolution was agreed to. The Conciliation Bill again came on for secondreading, but Mr. TOMLINSON was speaking in opposition to it when midnight arrived, and the debate had to be adjourned. The Committee on the Irish Land Acts was nominated just before tile House rose.

In the House of Lords the Limitation of Actions (Debt) Bill was referred to the Standing Committee on Law. The Land Transfer Bill was read the second time and the Solicitors' Examination Bill the first time. WEDNESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday, the second reading of the Miners' Eight Hours Bill was moved by Mr. Rom, who said the measure proposed to limit the hours of labour under ground to eight from leaving the surface to attaining it igain. The bill was rejected in 1892 by a majority of 112, but the next time it was dis- cussed it had a majority of 78. He did not think a general Eight Hours Bill was practicable, but in the coal trade, which was exceptional, he thought it a necessity. Mr. CREMER saeonded the motion for second reading. Sir A. HICKMAN moved the rejection of the bill, and Sir J. PEASE seconded the motion. Mr. T. BAILEY, in sup- porting the bill, said he was glad to sa) that all the colliery proprietors in the country were not opposed to the bill. Mr. T. W. Legh, Mr. G. Balfour, Mr.J. Wilson (Durham), and Sir J. Fergusson opposed, and Mr. Birrell, Lord R. Churchill, and Mr. J. K. Hardie sup- ported the bill. Mr. MATHER said his reason and his conscience revolted against supporting a bill which would make that House responsible in the future for matters which solely and wholly concerned employers and employed. He could not for the life of him under- stand why it was not possible in the great mining in- dustry of the country, which was far simpler in its character than the business in which he himself was engaged, for the masters and men to agree as to what in regard to hours should be the law and custom of the trade. He was strongly in favour of a raduc- tion of hours, but he desired that it should leave peace and contentment behind it, and not be a source of trouble in the future. Mr. ASQUITH said a very considerable majority of the members of the Government, himself included, were in favour of the bill and would support the second reading, but there were others who took a different view. He did net under- stand that the promoters of the bill would take up the short-sighted and foolish attitude of insisting upon the bill, the whole bill, and nothing but the bill, and decline to listen to reasonable proposals in Committee. Fer instance, he could not think it right that the penalty f, r contravention of the Act should fall upon the employer alone ; any person who violated the law ought to be made equally liable for the consequeness. On a division the numbers were—For the bill, 21; against, 194; majority, 87. The bill was then read a second time,and the Committee stage was nominally fixed for Monday next. Both the Merchandise Marks (Files) Bill and the Shops Early Closing) Bill were opposed, and in each case the motion for second reading had to be postponed.

ANARCHISTS AND THE ROYAL EXCHANGE. MURDEROUS THREATS.

Carnet, the Anarchist, who was arrested on Sunday, was brought before the magistrates at Bow-street Police Court on Monday, charged with being iu possession of explosives for an unlawful purpose. He made an ex.:ra- ordinary confession. He said he had intended to kji Inspector Melville, because be had arrested so many of his comrades. Then he should have had no compunction , in shooting half-a-dozen officers if they attempted to seize him. He went with Polti to give 4"-- orcler for the bomb, and it was his purpose to throw it into the Reyal Exchange. England was rich. At the Royal Exchange more rich people gathered together than at any other place, and there the explosion would have been the most effective. One or two of his comrades hid failed, but the third would succeed. He had no religion, but, wag determined to carry out his idea. In two or three years there would net be a Government in existence, here oz elsewhere. The prisoner was remanded.

THE EXAMINER,

-;ATURDAY, APRIL 29, IEI4

THE Bill for fixing the maximum ,Prs' Eight nours. days work of miners at eight hours was again read a second time in the HOMO of Corn mons on Wednes- day. Few questions have ripened, in the Parliamentary sense, more rapidly than this of the compulsory shortening of the hours of labour, and especially of the labour of miners. Two years ago this eight-hours Bill was rejected in the House of Commons by the sweeping majority of 112. The division of last year evidenced a re- markable change, for the second reading was then carried by a majority of 78. This year the majority is 87. It seems, therefore, as certain as anything can be in politics that the Miners' Eight Hours Bill will SOOn have the force and authority of law. It is said, of course, that the Bill will be considerably altered in committee, and some even go so far as to say that it will be "amended " almost out of existence. But there is no need to take prognostications of this kind too seriously. The miners cause has now many vigilant repre- sentatives in the House of Commons, and it may be confidently assumed that the same arguments and the same pressure .of public opinion which secured the second reading , will ensure the subsequent progress of the Bill. It is likely enough that the Bill in some of its pro- visions will be modifir_d, but there is now little fear of its being mutilsted in any of its essential parts. For one thing, the promoters of the Bill will deriv,s, great advantage from the sympathy and support of the Government. With one or two exceptions, the cabinet have, from the Prime ( Minister downwards,deelared themselves in favour of the measure, and every facility will be given for its passage. The opposition to the second reading on Wednesday followed the lines with which we are now all pretty familiar. Perhaps the strongest note of resistance caml from Mr. J. Wilson who represented the views of a portion of the miners of Durham and Northumberland. The position taken up by Mr. Wilson and those on behalf of whom he spoke is neither very logical nor very creditable. The miners of these two north- ern countries now work less than eight hours a day, yet they seem disposed, or at all events their leaders seem disposed, to stand in the way of the miners of other parts of the 'country enjoying a similar boon. And what is more, it appears that their short hours are obtained largely at the expense of the boys who are kept at work for Something like ten hours a day. To most people one of the strongest arguments for the present Bill is that it will prevent the over-work of juveniles for the benefit of their own parents, for that is What it means to a great extent in the colliery districts of the North. Another argument that was used in opposition to the Bill, was the very familiar one of foreign competition, and of the handicapping of home industries. It has been said that " Famil- iarity breeds contempt," and it is so in this case. The impending ruin of native industries has been so often foretold when there has been any sugges- tion of ameliorating the conditions of any section of the working classes, and these prophecies of evil have been so invariably falsified that argu- ments of this character now carry little or no weight. In fact, taking. the debate as a whole, it can scarcely be disputed that it was as decisively in favour of the Bill as was the division that followed.

MOST people have come to the COD- " Trickg elusion that there are trichs in the Trade."

milk trade, just as there are tricks in most other trades. The peculiarly thin and meagre consistenco of the fluid that is sometimes delivered to householders is suggestive of a certain amount of manipulation. But probably few of us were prepared for the revelations which an ex-dairyman makes in the "Economic Review "of the extent to which the public are imposed upon in regard to the nourishing and necessary milk they consume. It should, however, be stated that this particular milk dealer gathered his experi- ences in London, and perhaps it is only fair and charitable tO assume tat provincial vendors are less " knowing " and more scrupulous than their metropolitan brethren. The writer in question bought his business from a man of high loc,tl' reputation and a " regular at at a place of worship " in one of the London suburbs, and hav- ing paid a deposit of i'loo his initiation into the mysteries; of the busittess began. In the first place he was told that it was necessary to dye the milk before selling it. The dye eon_ sisted of a teaspoonful of liquid anatto with every eight quarts, and. this process was for the purpose of giving the milk a rich creamy appearance. What was known as " cooking butter" was pre_ duce(' by a plentiful admixture of margarine with genuine butter, and "nursery milk," of which seine dairies make a speciality, was simply ordinary milk put into sealed cans. He learned too that more milk was sold from the dairy than. was sent to it, but ho appears., to have been left to form his own conclusions as to how that little feat of legerde- main was performed. Another source of profit wis found in skimming the cream off one lat of milk and then nixing fresh milk with the skim milk. Then -there was the " new-laid egg" depitrtment which was not to be neglected. Tim eggs were bought in cases of 1,200 from FrAnce, and as it was presumed tint th cse en the -top were the freshest they were sold as the "new-lad "; the next layer were described as the "bes!.-, breakfast ;" and the rest wa..?.sue ply

'1 HE EN: A. MINES SATURDAY, APRIL 28, 1894.

"fresh." lii order to build up and extend a con- nection various expedients were resorted to, such as hribing care-takers and house agents' clerks in order to procure the names of new tenants who might be secured as customers. From all this it would seem that milk selling, in the metropolis at all events, is by no means the simple business that it might seem to be to outsiders. At all events the anonymous writer who makes these remarkable allegations declares that he found an experience of three months amply sufficient, and he sold out at a loss of half the capital he had expended in the purchase of the business.

WIDNES Y.M.C.A.

ONE of the latest American An Industrial Army. "notions" is a march of unem- ployed and discontented workthen upon the capital city, in order that the grievances of those who take part ix it may be impressively brought under the notice of Congress. Companies of, this great industrial army have been formed in various parts of the western States, and for a week or two past they have been converging upon Washington. At first little notiCe was taken of the movement, then it was followed with amused curiosity, but within the last few days the govern- ing and comfortable classes of the great Republic have viewed the development of affairs witli some- thing like alarm. The numbers of the " indus- trials " have rapidly increased, some of the larger cities, it is said, turning out recruits by the thou- sand. In some eases when railway companies refused to give the demonstrators a "free lift," trains have been seized and used to expedite the march eastwards. Deputy marshalls who attempted to repress lawlessness have been disarmed and ther weapons apPropriated. In fact, con- sidering the enormous nuuber of unemployed men, and the seething discontent and social un- rest that is apparent in the industrial regions of the States at the present time, the movement might easily be the starting point of a civil conflict of the most deplorable Kind. It is not surprising, therefore, that both the Federal and State Govern- ments are beginning to take thb matter very seriously, and are. calling out militia and regular troops. These vigorous measures will no doubt' repress the present disorders, but they will do so only by driving discontent beneath the surface. America has produced a great number of million- aires, but at the same time it has developed a social question of vast magnitude. Whatever may become of the present "industrial army" it is quite certain that the evils against which it pro- tests will not be abolished by the use or either Federal troops or militia.

WIDNES YOUNG PEOPLE'S SOCIETY OF CHRISTIAN ENDEAVOUR.

This Society held its weekly meeting on Monday even- ing when Mr. J. R. Routledge delivered an address on " Troyer."

The usual weekly meeting of this Society was held in Bedford Chambers on Monday night. An admirable address was delivered by the Rev. W. E. Lead (Primitive Methodist minister) on "How to study the Bible," and was greatly appreciated by all present.

ACCIDENT TO A SAILOR AT RUNCORN DOCKS.

A somewhat serious acc;dent happened on Friday after- noon of last week at the Runcorn Docks. Whilst Charles Rooney, sailor, of Ovel ton, Frodsham, was enga,ged in placing a" fender" over the side of the kessel Mary tlillsas she was leaving doek, he fell overboard between the wall and the vessel. He was badly crushed, and after having been bravely rescued from the water he was attnded by Dr. Robinson. Though admission would have bt en permitted to the Southern Hospital, Liverpool, the mother of the injured man requested that he should be taken home, which was accordingly com- plied with. MEETING OF TilE RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERARCE.

The fortilightly meeting of the above society was held on Saturday evening, the president, Mr. David Smith, oceilpving the chair. Mr. Thomas J. Bolt introduced ihe sulijeet oi •' Training the cadet (or Juvenile Order) in temperance principles." lie contended that it was imperative to train them at the regular meetings, not only as to the pernicious habit of smoking, but as to the properties and injurious effects .of drink to the human system. He advocated offering prizes to the best teci- ters as it would be it healthy stimulus to the members. Following the address was an interesting conversation by the undermentioned officers upon the subject : Messrs. John Jones, Edward Key, Daniel Bisbrown, and Thomas Co( ke. It was resolved that a copy or the "Son of Temperaecc " should be regularly supplied to the Free Library.

ALLEGED BIGAMY AT RUNCORN.

At the Runeorti Petty Sessions on Monday morning. before Me,srs. A. Thomas, S. Taylor and F. J. Norman, Mary Dagnall was charged with having feloniously married Jesse Duckers, on April 9th. at Runcorn, her fir,t husband, Wiiliain Dagnall being then alive ; and Jesse Dockers with aiding and abetting Mary Dagnall in the offence.—lnspector Cooper deposed to visiting the Parish Church, Runcorn, on the 9th inst., where, upon examining the registers, he found that the female prisoner had gone through the torn of marriage with William Dagnall, on May 26th, 1885 ; and with Jesse Duckers on the 9:.h April. When apprehended Duckers said he knew the woman Dagnall was married, but her husband had married her under a wrong name. A simi- lar statement was made by the woman after her arrest. SUDDEN DEATH AT WIDNES.

On Mon Jay morning about ten o'clock, a man named Michael Timmins, of 6, Canal-st.,was feund dead in bed. The deceased lett wsrk at noon on Sunday and had his usual meals and went to bed about nine o'clock. On Monday morning he was called, but receiving no answer the landlady went to his room and found him dead in his bed. Deceased made no complaint when retiring to bed, and appeared in his usual health. He had suffered from chronic bronchitis for several years.
