ritic NEW GENERAL DISTRICT RATE FOR RUNCORN.

A special meeting of the Runcorn Board of Conatnis- 'loners was held on Tuesday evening in the Board room for the purpose of affixing the seal of the Board to tho new general district rate. Mr. S. Taylor occupiod the chair and there were also present Messrs. F. Wiswall, E. unison, D. Brisbrown, J. Meadowcroft, W. Handley, F. A. Lake, 'law clerk ; E. Marshall, secretary; and M. Barker, surveyor.—Mr. W. Handley proposed "that the seal of the Board be affixed to the General District rate of 25. 10(1. in the £ for the year ending March 31st, 1895."—Mr. D. hisbrown in seconding said he'would like to bear his testimony- to the manner in which the Board had endeavoured to bring the rate down to its normal level. It was obvious to all that they had lad to drop a number of improvements in order to keep the rate at 2s. 10d.' and it was a wonder to him that it was not thanmore than that. There was no disguising the fact that their expenses were gradually creeping up, but he hoped that their future years would be more prosperous.—The proposition was unanimously carried. MIDDEN DEATH OF A CHILD IN RUNCORN.

A little girl named Ethel O'Donovan, aged two and a half years, who lived with her parents at 68, Grice's Square, Runcorn, died rather suddenly on Tuesday afternoon. About a month ago the child sustained a small wound above one of her eyes whilst .playing with some other children. After having been attend cd the wound apparently healed, but subsequently it leap. peared and the child became very ill. She was attacked with convulsive fits and was medically treated by Dr. McLennan up to the time of death. Mr. J. E. Viorseley conducted the inquiry this (Friday) morning at the Run- corn Police Court.—The mother of the child, Elin. O'Donovan, gave evidence, from which it appeared that she thought the wound was caused by the foot of a little boy striking her child in the eye whilst they were playing together on Una 29th February.—Dr. McLennan deposed to making a post-mortem examination, and ex- pressed the opinion that the cause of death was due to the existence of an abeess on the brain, caused by the child being struck by some sharp instrument.—A ver- dict of " Accidental death " was recorded.

RUNCORN PUBLICANS AND TklE INCREASED LIQUOR

DUTIES.

A special meeting of the Runcorn and District Licensed Victuallers Association was held on Teesday evening at the Na.vizaticn Hotel, Canal-street, Runcorn. Mr. John Coxfield occupied the chair, and there was a good attendance. The meeting was called for the pur- pose of discussing, with other business, the proposal of the Chancellor of the Exchequer to increase the ducks on beer and spirits. After several addresses had been delivered on the subject, it was propoeed, seconded and unanimously passed :—" That this meeting of the Run- corn Licensed Victuallers' Association hereby expresses its strong condemnation of the action of the Chancellor of the Exchequer with regard to his proposed increase of duties on beer and spirits, and respectfully asks him to reconsider that portion of his Budget, because, in spite of what may be said to the caterer v, it inflicts greatinjustice on the trade and consumer alike." It was also resolved to forward a cony of this resolution to Mr. J. T. Brunner, M.P. for this division, accompanied by a special request to oppose the imposition of the beer and spirit duties. This was all the business of a public nature.

AMALGAMATION OF THE RUNCORN CYCLING CLUBS.

An enthusiastic and well attended meeting of the members of the two cycling clubs took place this week at the Cocoa rooms, High-st., Runcorn, for the purpose of considering the questions of amalgamation. Mr. C. Le Couteur was voted to the chair, and the prospects of each organization were reviewed. After a little discus- sion it was unanimously resolved that the two clubs amalgamate, and adopt as its name the " Runcorn Cycling Club." The following officers were appointed : —Messrs. R. Moore, and P. Buckley, secretary, and assistant secretary ; Mr. T. Crawford, treasurer ; Mvssi a C. Le Couteur and Hindley captain and sub-captiin of Saturday riders ; Messrs. Winwards and Mack, captain and sub-captain of Wednesday riders ; commir( e : Messrs. Lawson, Rushton, Crimes, Taylor, Turtoti,Neild Withington, and Wiswall. It was further decided to adopt the monogram badge, and that the club start at 2-30 on Wednesday and 2-15 on Saturday from Devon- shire Square, on their weekly excursions. A number of other rules were drawn up and passed, and arrange- ments are being made for procuring medals which will be presented to those members gaining the best average attendance. On Wednesday theclub mustered in strong force in Devonshire Square, and indulged in the first run under the new arrangement, their destination being Delamere. The spin proved extremely enjoyable to all. An ex cellent tea was partaken of before returning home amusements of various kinds were participated in. The Saturday riders will also run to Delatnere,and next wetk the rendezvous of both sections will be Chester. DEATH OF THB REV. WM. DICK.

The Rev. William Dick, late pastor at Prescot, has died at Seacombe at the age of 89. Mr. Dick had been in the ministry for over fifty years,during which time he laboured hard in the cause of Tem- perance and social reforms. Mr. Andrew E. Dick, the deacon-secretary of Harecourt Chapel, and Mr. Dick of Bewsey-road, Warrington are sons.

Croft, cn Saturday afternoon, there were interred the remains of the late Mr. James Royle, of Chapel-lane, who died on Wednesday last week. A B UTCHER'S DISPUTE AT E.ARLESTOWN.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before His Honour Judge Wynne Foulkes, Richard Jones butcher, Earlestown, sued John Holland, butcher, Earlestown for .E 6 18s. 4d. money due for meat delivered, Mr. 11. J. Longton defended. The plaintiff said that the defendant had been a ms- tomer for some years, and he had run on the debt above mentioned. Mrs. Jones corroborated her hus- band evidence and said that the debt began to ac- cumulate in 1892. Cross-examined, each witness denied that they knew the defeiidant h d Oven up business in his wife's favour previous to 1892, and the debt for which he was sued was his wife s debt Plaintiff admitted that he had seen Mrs. tic:Hawn; name over the door. Mr. Longton scil th:it his client had given over the business to hizl wife, on,: was not aware of the debt. He had since taken the business again but did not wish to be encumbered by his wife's debts. After evidence by the defen- dant, his wife, and Thomas Lattimer, his Honour said that he would have given judgement for the plaintiff but for the fact of him seeing Mrs. Holland's name over the shop door, which should have caused him to make inquiries. He would therefore give judgment for the defendant but would not al:ow Costs.

I appeared to him (Mr. Voisey) that Farrell, the bank- rupt had been a mere fool in the hands of the solicitors who financed him through all his building transactions. lie kept no accounts and was quite unable to keep any, but trusted entirely to these solicitors for keeping all necessary accounts, and it had been his (the trustee's) opinion that be had all along signed without much, if any, inquiry, whatever, any deeds on papers these people have submitted for his signature. After his first assurance he was absolutely ignorant up to the date of Ws bankruptcy of being in any way connected as owner with the Farnworth property, and he (Mr. Voisey) never expected to find him able to give him any information whatever of how the purchase price for it was made up. The fact was he found the bankrupt's mind an utter blank upon it. The solicitors concerned, were at one time, Woodcock and Kirby, and after that firni'.4 dissolution, then either one or the other of them separately. Kirby (who afterwards became a c aiviet• prisoner) he (Mr. Voisey) questioned alaout the Fartiworth property, but the only replies he got w,r. that he knew nothing whatever of the transactions as to the property. The whereabouts of the other eentleman,'Woodcock, he had always been gives to understand was out of England. The trustee con- cluded 1-,y stating that in his opinion the bankrupt's ti -ables all earns about through his own simplicity of charact-r ; that he [146 had no guilty intent, and had received no benefit whatevLr from his building opera- tions beyond bare subsistence.

Mr. Linker, solicitor. Runcorn, • and Mr. Tucker, ono of the bankrupt's debtors, supported the applicatien for toe iiiseliaree of the bankrupt. 'His H-onour said there was no disputing the fact that the bankrupt had committed certain offences whichtwere of a grave nature. He had failed to keep proper ac- counts, and justices of eminence bad expressed the opin- ion, and he had the same opinion regarding it, that if people kept their proper bocls of accounts they would readily know when they were going wrong, and would cease to bring distress and ruin upon other people in being unable to meet their creditors. It was a serious tillence when a man went on trading when he knew, or should know, that he was insolvent. If he had kept proper hooks he would have very soon founci out that he was going .wrong, and when be had made that ditcovery he would have been able to pull up and-so prevented from bringing distress on other people. It appeared that the bankrupt had speculated. That was another offence he had cemmitted. He really began husiness without any capital whatever, and when a man commenced building without capital it meant that everything was done by borrowed money. He trusted to chance of circumstances to bring him safely through it. If it Jaded he became a bankrupt, if it succeeded he paid his creditors. The bankrupt had been made a tool, 14) some' extent, by Messrs. Woodcock and Kirby who have since been struck off the rolls. They made him file his petition in bankruptcy. He trusted to these men alt the way through and he was in their hands. There was this to be stated that Messrs. Woodcock and Kirby were not responsible in the first instance for the bank- rupt beginning without capital, and this speculation which he did carry on. The only part they played was that they financed him, and he must have known that be had no money of his own. Wits regard to the I).:z.itdous specWation cf the purchase of the Farnworth property, the ankrupt was not free from blame. He • could only regard him as one of those speculative builders who imperil all who do business with them. He there- fore thought that he ought not to discharge the bankrupt at once. He would suspend him for two years. Had it n•t been for the extenuating circumstances of the Case he would have adjourned it for fire years.

RUNCORN TONIC SOL FA CLASSES,

ANNUM. DISTRIBUTION OF CERTIFICATES, The annual ceremony of distributing the certifi- dfues to the successful students of the Rnacorn Tonic Sol Pa classe., which have been conducted for so many years with such singular success by Mr John liolford, L.T.S.C., took place on Monday evening in the Public Hall, Runcorn, when the poplar historical cantata" Magna Charta "was ad- mirably performed by a band and chorus, numbering 18.0 members,. There was a fairly large attendance 'though, perhaps, not so numerous as the event merited. The charecters, represented in the cantata were " Jus- tice," (reesontetecl) MiSS K. Edwards, soprano; " King ,intiti" Mr. Charles Leatilwood, bass ; and" Stephen Lengton," Mr. E. IVorrall. tenor; all of whom are members of the classes. With regard to the various perfortnaecee of the principals none but remarks of praise cite he ma Te. Miss Edwards, as "Justice," sang Nktti much pathetic feeling ; Mr: Charles Leathwood, in his representation of " King John," was very powerful and exp.tessive ; whilst Mr. E. Worrell made an ideal .• Stephen Laevon." Concerning the chorus, everyone who ita4 ncen fortunate enough to hear the singing of this combivation, must have ' been very favourably impressed. It may therefore suffice to say that as en previous occasions they sustained their respective parts in the most corn- , mendable manner, and received many expressions of , enthusiastic approval from the audience. The entertain- ment on the whole reflects great credit upon the way in which the students have been trained by Mr. Hoiford. At , the interval Mr. Holford expressed the great pleasure they all felt in having the presence of Mr. E. Tizley there that evening for the purpose of distributing , h., certificates. They considered thetnseives very highly tAvoured in having a gentleman like Mr. Tizley amongst them, occupying., as he did, the distinguished position of Chairman of the Manchester District Tonic Sol Fa Council. lie hoped Mr. Tizley would take back with him to Manchester a good impression of what he had heard in Ituncons. (Applause.) Mr. Tizley said that when Mr. Holford asked him to Wale to Runcorn for the purpose of distributing the , certificates he very gladly consented, because he had ! warmly sympathised with Mr. Holford in the good nth in which he was engaged, and which be believed, %yes being conducted on the right lines. So far, he was very pleased with tee choir, and he appealed to those ' musicians in the andience as to whether the perform- . %nee that evening was not distinctly creditable to the class, itonsiderine the extreme youth of some of the mtanta rs. He considered that the class, as an elemen- tary one, was di•tinctly above the average, which was extremely creditable to Mr. Holford. (Applause.) He went on to speak at considerable length on the progress which the art of music had made within the past twenty years, more especially amongst the working classes ; end also of the difference between the'staff notation anti the Tonic Sol Fa. For many years he had himself been a confirmed Solfaist, inasmuch as it gave clearer insight into music than he bad ever bad before he te came acquainted with it. He could not say that the Tonic was superior in every respect, but it was in many ways that the knowledge of the one en- abled them the better to understand the other. He also advised the I-moral-tea to learn the staff notation. When Mr. riolfero's choir visited Manchester to take part in the competition fe* expected them to get the third prize. OBSTRUCTING THE HIGHWAY. Thomas Pil kington con:raetor, Warrington was charged at Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday with having on the 26th March at Appleton obstructed the highway. Mr. A.Browne, solicitor, appeared for the de- fendant. P.C. Millington stated that betwten twelve and one o'clock a.m. on the (jay in question he saw a large truck containing a boiler on the Chester-road, Grappenhall, near to the Dog and Dart Inn. There was nobody in charge and it remained there all night. It took up three parts of the road and was very dangerous. Mr. Browne said the detondant Mr. Pilkington had un- dertaken to remove a Oiler from Wilmslow to Preston Brook. He commenced on Friday night, but instead of

it taking a few hours to get the boiler loaded it took nineteen hours, and the horses and men had been work- ing 48 hours when they reached Grappenhall. They had still seven or eight miles to do and Mr. Pilkington thought the men had worked long enough. The boiler weighed 21 tons and there being no place to put it, he thought it better to leave it on the road. He left a man named Robert Reytnond t) make arrangements with the police, and he stopped until one o'clock but he was un- able to see the constable. It wm a light night and the boiler could be seen 200 yards away. Ibbert Reymond corroborated Mr. Br() sne's statement, as to being in charge until crie o'ciock and being unah!e to tee *.lic con- stable. The defendant was fined the costs.

Osborne. of Ibbert-sereet, Halton-road, were charged with stealing 2 curry combs and two brushes valued ss. 3d. from the shop of Mr. Charles Dobson general dealer, Bridge-street, Runcorn, on or about the 13th of April, and further with receiving the same well knowing them to have been stolen.--A second charge was brought against the two female prisoners with stealing two brushes, and eight pairs of laces value 3s. 7d. on or about the 130 April from the shop of Mary Meadow- croft, Of 18. Church-street; and a third charge against Elizabeth Beers of stealing a velvet skirt value 54. the property of Jane Sole, 50,Regent-straet, in the month Of February last.—The prisoners who were represented by Mr. Joseph Burton, solicitor, pleaded " not guilty " to all charges.—Evidence of identification was given by the various supposed owners of the alleged stolen property after which Stirgeant Lea and P.C. Gregory testified to searching the house occupied by the prisoners in Robert- street, Halton-road, and to finding all the artieles secreted in diffesent parts of, the dwelling. When they qu, stioned Mrs. Osborne she replied that she had loinight three of the brushes, and the two curry combs in Manchester, but the other brush was given her by Mesqrs. Simpson and Davies for the purpose of tarring their e.u.al boat.. With regard to the skirt, Sergeant. Lea said t le+, when Beers Was apprehended she replied to the ch. ree that her mother had bought her the skirt at Shoop till Market, Manchester.—James Higgins, 'foreman in Messrs. Simpon and Davies' employ, de- posed to Mr. and Mrs. Osborne being employed by his finn on t.o ioats called "and " Midas.' It It WAS customary for these boat people to apply for gas-tar brushes when the boats required painting. He had given the prisoners an order for a gas-tar brush similar to that which they were charged with stealing. He could not however say how long ago the order was granted.—P.C. Gregory said that when be apprehended the male prisoner the latter said the articles were pur- chased by his wife in Manchester.—Mr. Burton in defending first submitted that no evidence had been adduci dto show that Charles Osborne was guilty. He therefore asked the Bench to discharge hitn.—The magistrates after some consideration arrived at the same conclusion and the male prisoner was accordingly discharged —Mr. Burton continuing his defence re- marked that the girl Beers was married but living apart from her husband. She had pleaded not guilty to the charges preferred against her concerning Mrs. Osborne she anti her husband had been in the employ of Messrs. Simpson and Davies for fifteen years and were honest anti hardworking. people. It was quite a common practice for fplks to go to Manchester and purchase such articles at the various markets at a cheap rate. There had never been any conviction against the mother and if the magistrates thought her guilty of the charges he hoped they would deal with her under the "First Offenders' Act."—Elizabeth Beers was committed to prison for twenty one days in each of the three cases, and the mother fined 104. in both cases..

ASSOCIATION. WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT CHALLENGE CUP.-FINAL TIE.

WARRINGTON V. Sr. HELENS LONDON AND NORTE-WES- TERE.—The final tie in the above competition was played off at Dalton Bank. on Saturday between War- rington and St. Helens. The homesters won the toss, and shortly after half-past three, Mr. W. Gill, of Grap- penhall kicked off. Connor scored after 20 minutes' play, amid general enthusiasm. For the remainder of the half St. Helens pressed a good deal, but the bome defence was quite safe. At half-time, the score was Warrington one goal, St. Helens nil. During the second half War- rington had the best of the game. Connor, who plated splendidly, scored two goals, and a third was heaced in by one of the visitor's backs. The other forwards played very fairly, and gave some good exhibitions of pusing. The final score was Warrington four goals, St. Helens nil. The Warrington team scored 18 goals against two during the whole competition. After the match Alderman Francomb, president of the Association presented the Cup and medals to successful players. The following were Saturday's teams :—Warrington : Goal, J. Forrest ; full backs, R. Evans, J. Mather ; half backs. J. Phillips, F. H. Williamson (captain), C. E. Linaker ; forwards, W. Reid, F. E. Norman, E. A. Connor, T. Gor- ton, T. Mather. St. Helens L. and N.W. : Goal, T. I O'Brien ; full backs, J. Moran, W. Chadwick; half backs, J. Taylor, H. Finney, W.. Richards ; forwards, G. ' RUGBY. WARRINGTON v. BROUGHTON RANGERS.

The home premier team were down in their list of fixtures to meet the Messley Club on Saturday, but as this organization recently became disbanded, the engage- ment could not be-fulfilled. So Warrington met instead, Broughton Rangers, who were disappointed in an en- gagement themselves. with Halifax, who had to go and take part in the Yorkshire Cup Final. The proceeds of the" gate" at Wilderspool were set apart for the assist- ance of the Borough Band, who have generously given their services at each match played at home during the season, and thus supplied a pleasant adjunct to the game. Unfortunately the "gate" was not so large as usual. The Rangers were well represented, but War- rington lacked the services of Heesom, Jclley, Thorniley and Nevins. The teams were of the following com- position :

Warrington : Boscow, back; Carey, Potts, Evans and Massey, three-quarters; James Bate and John Bate (cap- tain), half-backs; Fair. Turner, Taylor, Dakin, Buckley, Berry, Holmes. Bailey and Houghton, forwards. Brough- ton Rangers: Todd, back; Taylor, Whitehead, Chapman and Garrity, three-quarters; Thompson and Hosker, half backs; Medley, Oman, Grier, Shepherd, Nowell, Shaw, Arrowsmitb, and Sanderson, forwards. Referee, Mr. E. Flowers.

Chapman kicked off towards the pavilion goal, with a slight breeze at his back. A mull let in the Rangers at the onset, and though Potts, James Bate, and Carey cleared the Broughton men out for a while, judicious kicking by Chapman and Whitehead once more renewed the attack. A neat display of passing by the Rangers' backs saw Garrity improve his side's position, and though Boscow partially stayed a rush headed by Med- ley and Skidmore. James Bate gained capital relief with ' a tall kick, which Evans and Potts spoiled, Whitehead returning. Taylor, however, gave the visitors again the advantage, and following a round of passing started by Hosker, Chapman dropped a goal in smart fashion. Fol- lowing the re-start the locals ncw took up the aggres- sive, and should have scored in the first nsinule had care only been exercised. As it was, Whitehead intercepted a wild pass, and sharp cross dribbling by Medley, Shep- herd, and Oman gave Boscow trouble. A pretty display between the Bates resulted in the half-way flag being passed, and then Dakin, aliley, Holmes, and Berry rushed through the thick of their .opponents, but faulty fielding of the oval ronbed the Warringtonians of an ex- dellent chance of scoring. Exchanges between Taylor and Massey ended in the former's favour. Turner next made a bold bid from a line out, and when getting dan- gerous was hauled down by a host of opponents. A round of passing by the home backs saw Carey trying s short kick and follow-up with ill success. Hosker and Thompson changed the venue to half-way, but as speedy was the return to the attack, Dakin. Taylor, and Turner rushing the oval, and Todd saved. Evans met the drop- out in true style, and though Whitehead responded Boa- cow got out of a difficulty amidst ringing cheers. The game was taken under the Broughton posts, and James Bates almost got in after tricky work. Again there was a laxity of nntsh in another passing bout, and Chapman snapping up an opportunity eased the pressure consi- derably. Another minor was chronicled in favour of Warrington, aad then a spell of uninteresting play was relieved when Turner. got away from a line-out, and after partially clearing the opposition saw his pass not accepted. James Bate, John Bate, and • Carey next transferred with the same fate as preceding movements whilst Potts made a strong effort being well tackled by Todd. Hosker cleared the locals out, and a too vigorous kick by Ilzscow sent up another minor. , The Rangers enuld not make much headway against the resolute tactics of the Warring- tonians.and after Massey and Evans had kicked well play was on the Broughton line when half time ;cal signalled with Broughton Hangers leading by lour points ti, nil. On resuming Warrington early on attacked, and the Bates passed out freely, whil:t Evans and Potts re- peatedly made headw.iy. Fine kicking by Chapman and Whitehead realised the Warrington quarter 114g,but the locals, evidently nettled at being in a minority, forced the visitors back to their quarters, Berry and Holmes foiling Todd in responding to a kick by John Bate. A good kick by, Taylor (Rangers) was poorly res- ponded to by Carey, mid Grier made his mark in a favourable position, but Chapman's kick was charged by Holmes. and Warrington breathed more freely. Buckley and Turner took the game direct to the visitors' lines, and Carey had a fine opening after a pretty bout by the Bates. The pass was knocked on, and then came a movotonous spell of forward play close to the Rangers' line. One strong rush by the locals saw Taylor in pos- session, and•ht .succeeded- in safely grounding the oval, whilst cheers greeted -Potts when he successfully nego- tiated the place kick, and thus gave his side the lead. The Broughton men played up pluckily, and came very near scoring on two occasions. soon after the kick off, but eventually Warrington again attacked, Todd being especially lucky to stave off efforts by Bailey, Carey,and James Bate. A fine round of passing started by the home skipper saw Massey making tracks for the line, whilst Evans essayed a shot at goal,the oval going wide of the desired mark. Give-and-take play followed, and though Warrington had a couple of free kicks, nothing substantial accrued, and the end came with Warrington victors by a goal (five points), to Broughton Rangers a dropped.gnal (four points). RUNCORN V. WIDNES.—A hotly-contested game bet- ween these rival teams took place on Saturday afternoon on the ground of the latter, before 5,000 spectators. The kiat off at the commencement and after the interval was performed by Mr. Henry Nibart, of the " Royal Diverce" Company. As in previous matches the visitors maintained their supremacy, forcing the visitors to take up a defensive game. The first and only goal was scored in the first half by Butterworth, although the Widnes men tried their utnaost to equalise their efforts were fruitless, and the game ended in a win for Runcorn by one goal to nil.

RUNCORN "A" V. WIDNES "A."—These teams engaged in a very close contest again on Saturday, the venue be- ing the Irwell-lane enclosure. The home combination was comparatively weak owing to several of the players , being unable to play. On the other hand,Widnes brought 1 a fairly representative team. The nature of play in the first half was rather slow, although on the part of both teams some pretty passing was seen. At half time the score was—Widnes one goal, Runcorn one try. On the resumption of hostilities the homesters gradually be- came the aggressors, and towards the finish they were thoroughly masters of the field. The final score was— Runcorn three tries, Widnes one goal. BIREENREAD WANDERERS "A " V. SOCIETY FOR THE PREVENTION OF CRUELTY TO CHILDREN.

WARRINGTON BRANCH.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Branch of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children, was held at the Town Hall, on Thurs- day last week, the Mayor (Alderman E. Greenall) presining. There were also present the Rev. Benjamin Waugh, the Rev. F. W. Willie (Rector), Captain Rodgers, Mr. T. Birchall, J.P., Mr. W. B. Greener, (hon. secretary), and a large number of ladies.

The MAYOR itnopening the prcceedings said that he had been asked to preside in the absence of Mr. Charlton Parr, who was advised by his medical atteedant not to attend any public meetings for some time. He(the Mayor', felt the greatest pleasure in being of use, no matter in what capacity,to such an admirable and Christian insti- tution as that in connection with which they were , assembled that evening. Be very much regretted that at the present day such a society should be absolutely necessary for the safety and protection of children who were subject to the cruel treatment of unnatural parents. It was two and a half years since that branch of the Society was formed but he could safely say that the good work it had been able to accomplish would repay, tenfold, the labour and time which many of those present had kindly devoted to it. (Applause). Mr. W. B. GREENER, the hon. sec., next read the annual report. It stated that the number of complaints dealt with during the year was 114, and involved the welfare of 300 children. The complaints were classified under the following heads :—General illtreatment, 5 ; assault, 10; starvation, 1 ; neglect, 87 ; abandonment, 2 ; exposure, 3 ; begging cases, 6 ; dangerous perform- ances, 0 ; immorality, 0"; other wrongs, 0. The cases were dealt with as follows :—Prosecuted, 21 ; convicted, 20 ; discharged, 1 ; earned, 83; otherwise dealt with. 6 ; dismissed, 4. The sentences inflicted ranged from one day's imprisonment to three months' imprisonment. The total number of cases investi- gated was 300, and the number of children . found to be insured was 114. During the year a branch of the Children's League of Pity had been formed in Warring- ton, and they had to-thank Miss Broadbent for the every energetic and successful way in which she started the work. The total revenue of the branch for the year was £416 ss. 31., and the expenditure £233 4s. "11d. The branch was thus enabled, by a gift from Mr. Henry Thornton, to forward on_ to the General Committee. £lB2 14s. 2,l.—The report was. adopted. • The Rev. BENJAMIN WAttlan next delivered a most eloquent address on behalf of the Society. He mentioned a number of Bills which he intended to esideaveer to get through Parliament, for the purpose of securing to chil- dren that protection which the adults of this kingdom have long enjoyed. The chief of these measure's, upon which the rev, gentleman spoke, was for the formation of a special court for dealing with juvenile dilinquents. In this direction the speaker made out a strong case against the methods which were now in vogue.of dealing with juvenile defaulters. He pointed out that in the first place it was a farce to place a child in a court intended for adults ; where it .could riot possibly understand the .form of procedure which was gone through, and where it was expected to answer questions • couched in the vague phrasiology which even puz- zled grown-up poi According to an Act passed he Charles 11, the Crown waa constituted the defender of all juvenile defaulters. But what did they find the clerks in their police court reading, in their mumbling and incoherent style ? If a child was the defendant the charge opened with "The Queen. v. Thomas Hardy." He contended this was a !ibel on the Queen's name. Their whole legal system of dealing with children was wrong; it was cruel and absurd. A child who was so young as to be incapable of having a true conception of right and wrong, ought not to be punished according to the value of the article stolen, but according to the feel- ings of the heart which prompted the act. A hungry child might steal a piece of bread, it Was arrested and detained in prison ; it might steal from a stall an orange or a flower, for which it had a 'fondness, it was arreqed and detained in prison. Or a child in a misehiev.ius moment might bra ak a wiedow, it was treated the 4,b:rie —treated as though it were a felon—placed in the same cells and docks as a murderer, or a criminal of the blankest type. And all this because it had broken a piece of glass. Glass of course 'cost money a child's heart cost nothing, and the law did not mind breaking it to punish the breaking of the glass. When 'a child was placed in the dock,there was no one to prove it innocent. The Crown,according tea perverted Is w.wac there to prove it guilty, and often did so unjustly. Only a few weeks ago he had proved that a child, who was detained in prison three days, and then ordered to NI flogged for alleged theft, was . innocent. A few weeks ago a child was arrested,or stealing two pots of fivers from a gentleman's garden was confined six days in a jail before being tried, and then evidence came out to the following effect. The chil ?e, was of an impression able nature, and finding a lost k itten, took it home nu of pity. During the night the animal died. The poe child buried it the following morning, and then brisk througn a fence and stole the flowers to plant on th kitten's grave. Instances on a parallel with this were to be found in hundreds, and when thee cnntemplated them what a hideous cruelty it seemed to place those innocent children in the position of felons just because they had acted upon the prompting of the heart, or upon childishly conceived ideas of justice. This Was one of the things they wanted altering more than anything else and he hoped soon to see a special court, called perhaps the House of-Justice for children, where the magistrates would act as protectors, on behalf of the Qusen. What. prevented them getting these measures passed at present was the great party friction in the House of Commons. Politicians did not appear to be there for the good of the country, but simply to oppose each other. They fought. mutually to extinguish each other like Kilkenny eats.— And-he hoped God would prosper them in this—(Laugh- ter)—and then they would get new men and have some work done. (Applause.)

A RUNCORN BUTCHER IN TEE POLICE COURT.

Thomas Clough, butcher, of Church-street, Run- corn, was charged on Monday morning at the Run- corn Petty Sessions with unlawfully acting in contravention of an order made under the Contagi- ous Diseases Animals Act 1878, by removing three fat pigs within an infected area, otherwise than in accordance with a licence granted to him for that pur- pose.—P.S. Lea said that according to the order all animals coming from public sales were removed in accordance with a licence granted which stated whether they bad to be slaughtered or otherwise. On the nth April, defendant attended a sale at bole, near Chester, and bought three pigs. He afterwards described them as "fat " to Inspector Tinchard who gave him a licence to remove them to the Top Locks slaughter-house, Run- corn. The nest morning witness received an order stating that such a licence had been granted to Mr. Clough and asking witness to see that the animals were slaughtered. He and P.C. Gregory saw Mr. Clough who told them that the pigs were at the Top Locks slaughter-house, but on going thither no pigs belonging to Mr. Clough were there. They then went to Mr. Clough's own back yard in Mill-street.where the animals were found in different styes among a number of other store pigs. When Mr, Clough was questioned he said that he wanted to get home because his horse had cast a shoe and was footsore. He would have taken them to the slaughter-house but for this occurrence. The ani- mals instead of being fat were quite lean.—Defendant denied having, told the officers that the pigs were at the Top Locks slaughter-house saving that he was not asked where they were. He did not think he was doing wrong by taking them to his own yard for a short time. Ple intended to send them akerwards to the Top Locks slaughter house.—P.C. Gregory corroborated the state- ment of PS. Lea.—Superintendant Atherton said that the animals were described as " fat " by the purchaser and the licence ordered them to be slaughtered in four days. The licence was made out on the buyer's word that the animals were fat, and that person he presumed was Mr. Clough. The Pigs were not fat but lean. They were evidently not bought to be killed in so bhort a time but were intended for store stock..--Defendant was fined 40s. ird 12s. Bd. costs or one month's imprisonment.

PEN KETH.

Tun Lusenat. CL.—The annual general meeting ot the above club was held on last evening week, in the eteb-room. There was a good attendance of members, Mr. J. B. Aitken in the chair. The minutes of the last general meeting were read by Mr. Fred Hughes, secre- tary, and passed.—The next business was the readinz of the statement of the club's accounts for the year 1893-94 by Mr. A. J. White, the treasurer. The statement showed a balance in hand,after meeting all the liabilities. Th:3 accounts were passed as submitted.—A vote of thanks to the retiring officers for their services to the club and party during the past year, was proposed by Mr. 'l'. Bell, seconded by Mr. W. T. Wilkinson, and carried unanimously.—Mr. J. B. Aitken was re-elected president, Messrs. H. Garnett, G. H. Bolton, J. Peers, C. E. Parker, D. Gam, tt and H. Hughes, vice-presidents ; and the names of three other gentlemen were proposed as vice-presidents provided their consent could be obtained. Messrs. F. Hughes and A. J. White were re-elected secretary and treasurer respectively, as also was Mr. J. Lewis as assistantsecretary. The committee were elected as follows. viz :—Messrs. Longshaw, H. Wilkinson, Cross, A. Wilkinson, A. Seed, T. Bell, King„ Humphries, T. Seed, and G. Wilson.—Mr. Aitken pro- posed a vote of confidence in the Government, which was seconded by Mr. F. Hughes, and carried unani- mously. It was decided to send a copy of the formal resolution agreed to, to Lord Rosebery and Sir W. Har- court. This concluded the public business. A vote of thanks to Mr. Aitken for his services in the chair was proposed by Mr. J. Lewis. seconded by Mr. H. Wilkin- son, and carried.—Mr. Aitken returned thanks, and the proceedings terminated. LOCAL TRAVELLERS.—Mr. Robert Garnett. J.P., of Hall Nook, and family, returned on Sunday morning from a trip to the Holy Land. Mr. C. E. Parker also landed on the shores of America on Sunday morning.

WARRING-EON MUNICIPA.L

FINAI CE.

A REPLY TO THE " CRITICISMS AND

SUGGESTIONS."

An "Examiner" reporter one day this week called upon Councillor J. Shaw Green, Deputy Chairman of the Finance Committee of the Warrington Cor- poration to ascert alit his opinions of the interview which we published last week under the title of "Warrington Municipal Finance: Some Criticisms and Suggestions." " Personally " said Mr. Conn. Shaw Green" I am very pleased that anyone outside the Council,sitould take an interest in the finances of the borough, for it is a department that has nothing inviting about it to induce ratepayers to go into details." "Do you think the suggested scheme for cable roads is feasable?" " I don't think it is. But I think that a tram lii, could be established, not running through the cen- tre of the town, and profitably worked. Witness the great numbers of people who uow ride to Latch- ford, to the Cemetery, and to tockton heath. think also that the correspondence system would work well. I have seen it in successful operation elsewhere." "Should the Corporation do this ? " "I am of opinion it would be far better done by a private Company than by the Corporation." It is suggested that the Corporation should take down the property in the narrow streets, so as to widen thew, and to build larger and more commo- dious erections. Could this be done?" . .

" My opinion is that it is altogether impracticable for us to undertake work of such magnitude. War- rington is an old town and its streets are narrow and contracted and not straight and some day a large sum of money will have to be expended in simply widening them; but I am of opinion that the burdens at present pressing upon the town are more than sufficient to justify a postponement of title neessary work." "May .1 a 6 a are you one of the people who would think the expenditure involved in building new premises upon the widened Streets suicidal?" " Well, I wilt hardly go so far, but I think the scheme is more a pleasant dream than a possible reality." " have you studied the paragraph which refers to the rate of interest paid. It is stated, you will observe that the Corporation is payinLf interest on loans at 4 per cent. and investing at 21 per cent." " It is true that, out ot the hail million Of so we owe, a sum of .V2OO is still bearing interest at 4 per cent. It is all old loan arranged a great many years t 4.0 when the current rate of interest was 4 per cent But this particular one is the last and expires in one this Near.. The rate per cent. upon all other louts is 3 and 31. it ic also absurd to say that we are investing money at 2i per cent. A very small stun of money, being the proceeds of the sale of a i,lot of land to the Infirmary Committee was required by the Local uoverument Board to be invested in consols, ant it is our only investment of the kind. We have, however, some portion of I he Police Superannuation Fund int ested upon the securities of other Corporations at ,3 per cent.." "it was stated in the interview that we published last week that the borough was not assessed at a sufficient amount, and certain figures ,a ere .quoted as to the, amount the assessment should be. What do you think of that?" " Nothing at all, those figures are assumptions only. The assessments are made by the Assessment Committee of the Board of Guardians. I have some knowledge of these matters, and I venture to say that they are based upon the actual letting values of the property in Warrington." " What do von think of the proposal to consolid- ate the debt .?" _

_ " There is no doubt that it is possible. When the Gas Works waspurchased the various loans for mak- ing the sewers were consolidated, and the Corpora- tion is now preparing a scheme for, as far as pos- sible, re-arranging the loans, and it is no secret that we hope to fund the whole of our indebtedness,and issue a debenture stock. IL wa, suggested in the in- terview which you published that the whole of the loans be paid off and borrowed at smaller rates. From this I gatnered that the gentleman, whose views you published, has not sufficaently studied the annual rep)rt, or he would find that a very large portion of the borough debt has been taken by way of annuity. This cannot, be repaid except, upon terms to be made with the holders.; and great, caution in dealing with such investments is nee- ne-sary. But the Finance Committee is thoroughly wide :Lwake to all this." _ .

" What about the proposed extension of time for the repayment of the loans ?" 4' Again I perceive that the gentleman whose views you published is not fully familiar with the way in which those things are arranged, and he specially quotes our water works purchase as not leaving a eufficiently long time to repay the capi- tal. Parliament, or the 14cwal Government Board deternatnes tne period within which all loans shall be repaid, and it may interest your readers to know that We made a big fight before the committee of the House of Commons on the purchase of the Water IVorks, and the utmost we could gat was GO years with relief for the first five years." In the interview it was stated that Bradford obtained 108 years to repay the loans on their water and gas works," 4' I am sorry that I have not more information on that point. But from the report I see that it was carried through in 1881, and there must have been very exceptional circumstance attending the appli- cation, for know the Local Government Board is now keeping a strict hand upon these matters. The comparison between English and American towns is scarcely applicable.. We are subject to authority and cannot take liberties with the finances of the Corporation. Warrington is in the proud posi- tion of bein,4 ;ade to say it has never advertised for money, nor yet paid a commission upon its loans, and your readers may rest assured that so far as safe guarding their interests is concerned, the Finance Committee has upon it men who are cap- able and willing to iflve, their hest energies and services, in promoting the true interests of our town."

MAYORAL "AT HOME" AT WARRINGfON.

The Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman E. and Mrs. Greensll) held an "at home " t the Town Hall oa Friday, the occassion bAng their first public appearance since their return from the South ot France. The attendanoe is compu,ed to be the largest at any similar function held during the Mayor's three years of office. The reception was held from three o'clock to six, and during that time Mr. and Mrs. Greenall were the recipients of warn expressions of welcome, after their pro- tracted absence. The Council Chamber was used as the reception roam. Many of the apartments of the Town Hall had been specially fitted up and draped by Messrs. S. E. and R. Johnson, and were placed at the disposal of the guests. The hall had been beautified by floral and plant decoration, and during the evening it was occupied by a band under the conductorship of Mr. R. 11. Pearson, who played selections of music of an appropriate character. Refreshments were supplied under the superintendence of Mr. Stanley Higham. CABINET PENSIONS.

When an ex-Minister applies for a political pension, says " Cassell's Saturday Journal," he makes a declara- thm to the the effect that his private means are not suffi- I cient to enable him to maintain his position. The amount of the pension varies according to the rank the recipient has held. A person who has filled the office of First Lord of the Admiralty, or any other political office , to which a salary of not less than £5,000 a year is at- tached, may, after four years' service receive a pension £2,000 per annum. An ex-Minister who has held a posi- tion worth less than £5,000 but more than £2,000 a year for six years, may be granted a pension of £1,200, while in the case of a person who has received more than £l,OOO but less than £2,000 ten years' service is required below he is entitled to a pension, the amount of which cannot exceed £BOO. Lord Georze Fiamiltm and Lord Cross have each a pension of £2,000 and Mr. Childers has also an annuity nominally of the same amount, but in the case of the latter, the amount of an allowance paid him by the colony of Victoria is deducted. Sir Michael hicks-Beach receives £1,200 a year, and Mr. Shaw-Lefevre formerly received a political pension, but relinquished it on an improvement taking place in his private means.

Read the following Correspondence. Ztr. John Harrop, Bridge Street, Warrington, 4th November, 1893. Dear Sir,—The guarantee on gas account havmg expired, we have to request a renewal of same. Your cheque for £5, for which we pay interest at 4 per cent., will meet the case. Kindly give the matter your prompt attention. Yours truly, F. TAYLOR.

Mr. John Llarrop, Bridge Street, Warrington, 12th March, 1894. Dear Sir,—As no notice ha; been taken of our letter 4th November, re deposit LS, you must accept the responsibility of the gas being turned off on Friday next, unless paid. Yours truly,

F. TAYLOR.

18th March, 1891.

Dear Sir,— have been consulted by Mr. John Harrop of Bridge Street,in this town,with reference to his being informed that unless a deposit of £5 be made with you before to-day, the gas would be cut off from his !chop in in Bridge-street. Be objects entirely- to be subjected to annoyance of having to pay a deposit, inasmuch a, be has been informed that none of his wholesale cus- tomers have been requsted to make a deposit, and inas- much as he employs over 500 hands at. his Bury-street Mills, in Stockport, and has branch shops at Stockport, Oldham, Hyde, Rochdale, and other towns, be declines to make any deposit. He, therefore instructs me to a.,lt you to put this letter before your Committee, when I am sure they will come to the conclusion that there is no necessity for the deposit to be made. Yours trult-, Mr. Frederick Taylor. ARTHUR BROWNE. Sec. to the Gas Committee.

Verbal Reply to the Letter of the 16:h inst. The Secretary of the Gas Committee waited on Mr. Browne, and from what transpired then, Mr. Browne was under the impression that nothing further wouhl be heard of the deposit. If you will refer to Sec- tion 11 of the Gas Works Clauses Act, 1871, you wit: find that we can require security to be given for gas supplied to any owner or occupier of premises sill hin the limits of supply. lam instructed by the Committee to state, that unless a deposit of £5, or a sufficient secu- rity, be given for the payment of any sum which may become due for gas supplied to your client, the Corpora- tion will ot once discontinue to supply gas to your client's premises on Wednesday next, if the deposit or security is not given in the meantime. YAurs truth-, J. LYON WIIIITLE.

VACCINATION :-IMMUNITY OF NURSES.

• ----- Sir,—lt is often urged that the immunity which is alleged of the nurses in small-pox hospitals is due and can only be due to their vaccination, But what is this WA read in the third report, of the Lord's Committee on the Metropolitan Hospitals? On page lxii. 345, we find : —The nurses are engaged on a three years' agreement. Their health is said to be good, and not one hts died within the last ten years. Their number is about twenty. And so also in the course of the evidence we have Dr. Hepwood, Resident Medical Officer of the London Fever Hospital, stating, Q. 21645. "Do you know what the mortality amongst nurses has been in the last ten years ? There has been no one who has died." Now, if vaccina- tion, and nothing but vaccination can save the nurses 1 from small-pox, what was it that saved these nutaws so ! completely from allthe various kinds of fever to which they were exposed? The medical authorities, however have never at emo- ted to explain how it is that hundreds of small-pox hos- pital nurses who had repeatedly declined vaccination in the Paris and Dublin Flospitals,no Case of unrevaceinattd small-pox was discovered ; and there are plenty (it small-pox hospital nurses to-day who have never hpen vaccinated and they enjoy a perfect immunity frrun the disease. Bat, ef course, there are odd cases that become puhiic property and I find in looking over my vaccination literatur,that during the Leicester small-pox of 1892-93 there were no leas than five nurses and attendants sf the small-pox hospital who had been attacked by small-poa, four hin vaccinated and one successfully revaccinate:l ; wit!, (it these a very bad case died of confluent small-pox. WARRINGTON POLICE COURT AND PRISON GATE MISSION. ANNUAL MEETING :—THE RECTOR AND THE BREWERS. In the Council Chamber of the Town Hall on Wednesday afternoon, the annual meeting of the Warrington Police Court and Prison Gate Mission was held. There was a very small attendance. Alderman Francomb presided and was supported by the Rector (Rev. F. W. Willis) and the Rev. J. Hirst, the diocesan secretary of the Mission. The CHAIRMAN in opening the proceedings said that he was not surprised to see such a small attendance,because in Warrington they were not used to holding meetings iri the middle of the day. He was quite sure that the institution in connection with which they had met, was doing a most useful work. He had the honour—and be s f ten thought the misfortune—of being a magistrate for some seven or eight years aed he had seen some very painful cases in his experience on the Bench. He thought it was of very little use for the Bench to lecture the people who came before them as a rule they only required to get out of the court, and would often assume either a callous air or one of bravado. This, however, soon disappeared when they were no longer before the peblic ; when they were in conversation with the Missionare, Mr. Taylor, and accepting the assistance or advice which he offered. He had often been extremely pleased to see the signs which passed between Mr. Taylor and the police, and often between the prisoners, for it showed hew earnest and genuine were the efforts which were being made to alleviate the vices and conse- quent sufferings which the poor were subject to. At one time lie remembered Warrington became quite notorious, chiefly through one of the Liverpool papers, for "rows" emote,' young women. At that time they were troubled with about 12 young women of a reckless character,who were no sooner out of prison than they were in again. These people no longer troubled the Bench, and this was almost wholly due to the inflteence of the Missionary. The Rev. F. W. WILLIS in addressing the meeting said there was no doubt that drink was the gteat cause of the crime and misery which they saw around them, Remedies were necessary, hut he objected to the remedy generally advocated, namely the abolition of drink. As far as he was concerned—and he wished to speak his mind—if he thought the drink traffic was wrong he would have nothing whatever to do with it ; but he did riot think it wrong. They were accused in the Church of being tongue-tied, and being afraid to discuss the sub- ject. It had been said that the Church was supported by beer, and he had seen in the papers some remarks about donations to the Parish Church being received from local brewers. He did riot deny for a moment the truth of this statement. He was only thankful to acknowledge publicallv and gratefully the help he had received from Sir Gilbert Greenall and Sir Andrew Walker. He would not have accepted a penny from them if he had thought the drink traffic wrong. But he did not think it was wrong, and he therefore was net ashamed to receive the gifts from the brewers. He looked upon the drink trade from the highest view ; he believed that the Bible from beginning to end allowed the moderate use of intoxicating drink. Our Saviour gave it in large quantities, not for religious purposes, hut for enjoyment, and wine was allowed in the Holy Communion. He therefore contended that drink was not \Trams, and he repeated that he was net ashamed of receiving assistance from brewers. He would by no means be prepared to go in for the abolition of the drink traffic. The remedies which he advised were : The re- duction of the number of pulelic houses, the shortening of the hours of sale, the abolition of shop keepers licenses and the restriction of the licensing of clubs. He would even go further and if it could be carried out, prevent. the consuming of drink on the premises in which they were sold, or at any rate, if consumed on the premises only in conjunction with food. If the public houses carried out their true purpose they would combine the sale of drink with the sale of food. There was a great deal of drunkenness caused by people being allowed to drink as much as they pleased in the public house. He also advocated that as many institutions as possible— such as the one with which they were connected—should be formed to prevent the abusive use of intoxicants. Miss BESSIE Durros addressed the meeting, and spoke of the excellent work which the Mission was doing. She was pleased to be connected with such a noble cause, and she was aware of the Christian work !which ,the Missionary was doing in Warrington. Mr. H. TAYLOR, Police Court Missionary,in the course of his remarks upon the work done in Warrington, read a number of letters from reformed offenders, speaking of the benefits they had derived from the advice and hells given them on timely occasions by the missionary. Although he did not take the credit of the chancel to him- he could assure the meeting that the sort of offenders referred to by the Chairman, who had troubled the Bench regularly, were now unheard of in the Police Court. The meeting was afterwards addressed by the Rev. J. Hirst, and concluded with the usual votes of thanks.

BELSBY.

-- -- THE FATAL RAILWAY ACCIDENT.—Mr. H. Churton, coroner, held the inquest on the body of Joseph Light- foot, aged 36, platelayer, who was accidently killed at He!shy on Thursday, the 19li inst. by being knocked down and run over by a goods train. The evidence adduced showed that the deceased was at work, with other men, on the line near Helsby station at 6.30 in the morning. He was standing on the line from Warrington to Chester when seeing a goods train approaching he stepped upon a siding. At the time an engine was shunting some waggons upon the siding, and by these he was knocked down and run over. His left leg arid arm were cut off and serious injury was done to his right leg. Dr. Bryant speedily attended him but at 8.30 the man died.—The jury returned a verdict of "Acci- dental Death."—The remains of the deceased man were interred on Saturday afternoon in the burial ground attached to the Efelsbv Church in the presence of a large number of villagers, who had been personally acquainted with the unfortunate young man during his life-time. Many expressions qf sympathy on behalf of the bereaved aged mother who, by the death of her son, has been deprived of the only means of support. A number of railway employes carried the coffin to the grave, the procession being headed by the members of the local lodge of the Ancient Order of Druids. Numerous floral tributes were laid upon the TEE RATING Or fiEr.,isy.— At the Frodsham Petty Sessions on Wednesday Messrs. C. Reynolds and A. Thomas sanctioned the kruy of 11d. in the £ as a new poor rate for the lnwn.hip. MI:, amount is estimated to be equivalact to £343 10s. during the year. EXTENSICN OF THE NATIONAL SCHOOLS.

An interesting event in the history of Helsby took ()lace on Saturday afternoon last when Mr. James Reynolds, J.P. performed the important ceremony of laying the foundation stone of the proposed addition to the National Schools. The improvements which the schools are undergoing are very extensive, and consist of the erection of a new infants' schoolroom ; the con. version of the master's house into class rooms. New offices are being supplied, playgrounds for boys and girls (given by the Marquis of Cholmondeley) are being pre- pared, and the drainage of the whole building to be im- proved. These alterations are all being carried out in accordance with the requirements of the Educational Department and are estimated to cost £BOO. The sum of £550 has already been subscribed towards the cost, the foil wing donations having been received :—Mr. J. B..ynolds J.P., the Telegraph Works Company, the Dio- eesee Society and the Misses Wright, £5O each ; the Joint Lines, and Miss Garrett, £25 each ; Mr. Hayes,the Quarry Works, and the National Society for the Spread of Education, £2O each. Mr. Samuel Davies, M.S.A., of Frocisham, has designed the improvements and his plans have been approved. Mr. John Littler, Frodsliam is the contractor for the work. In the afternoon a pro- cession was formed and headed by the Helsby Brass Band marched through the village to the church where a service was conducted and a brief address delivered by the Yen. Archdeacon Barber. Subsequently the congre- gation repaired to the school where the stone-laying was performed by Mr. J. Reynolds amidst a large number of interested onlookers. Amongst those present being the Venerable Archdeacon B Artier (Caester), the Revs. W.T. Vale (vicar of Helsby), 11. B. I3logg (vicar of Frodsham), F. S. Banner (Dunham-o'th-Hill), D. Charley (vicar of Ice), W. S. M. Bennett (Chester) ; Colonel Ashton (Frodshanr, Messrs. H. Tiley, S. Davies (Frorishane), Lowe, J. Jeffs, Thomas Tudor (churchwardens), William Mc Walters, John J.Garner (sidesmen),and Hill. A silver trowel bearing an inscription commemorative of the event was presented by the Rev. W. T. Vale, on behalf of Mr. Samuel Davies, the architect to Mr. Reynolds. In making the presentation,the rev. gentleman said that the extension would provide accommodation for 115 extra scholars. Mr. Reynolds had assisted them nobly in every good work they had taken in hand, and they all hoped that the warm weather might have the effect of etrenethening him constitutionally and that he would be spared for many years to came. Mr. J. Reynolds having declared the corner stone " well and truly laid," the Vev. Archdeacon Barber addressed the gathering at some considerable length. He expressed a hope that they would remember the advise given by the Bishop of Gloucester a few month.; ago and "Stick to their Church schools."—Upon the motion of the Rev. H. B. Bloeg, a le arty vote of thanks wa4 accorded the Yen. Archdeacon Barber and Mr. James Reynolds for the part they had taken in the proceedings that afternoon.

MR. JABEZ BALFOUR.

It seems almost certain that Mr. Jabez Spencer Bal- four will this time escape the hands of justice. The Argentine judge, to say the least, did not take kindly to the British demand. Jabez has plenty of cash, and now that he is released from prison on the ground of " ill- h6alth" he has lost all his proverbial sharpness if he does not make a complete escape of it. There may still be a chance for the relisation of Mr. Stead's dream that Jabez may yet turn over a new leaf through the influence of the Salvation Army at Buenos Ayres. Unlikely dream to come true, though.

RATEPAYERS MEETING AT HAYDOCK.

THE QUESTION OF WARDS.

A very large meeting of the ratepayers and house- holders in Ilsy,lock township was held in the Col- liery Schools, llaydock on Wednesday evening to take into consideration and decide upon the ques- tion of dividing the townships into wards. 31 r. T. D. Grimlte-Drayton presided, and he was supported, on the platform by Mr. Charnock (solicitor) and the members of the Haydock Local Board. Mr. J. Matthews read the notice convening the meeting after which

The Chairman apologised for the absence of the Law Clerk, Mr. Ellis, who unfortunately had been kept in town, but he had asked Mr. Char-flock to attend the meeting, and if there was any legal advice they waned they would get it from him. It was common know- ledge with all of them that under the new bill which had been passed, that the townships of Havdock would no longer have a Local Board. .le, would be divided and made into a District Council. In this case, it was considered desirable it should tie divided into wards, and if they adopted that now, it would save ex- pense. They would have seen by the papers that while the greater majority of the Local Board were strongly in favour of dividing the district into three wards with three members to each ward, some other members of the Board, objected to that, and said they wanted 12 mem- bers., So far as his personal experience was concerned, he must confess that he never found a committee work worse for being small. To his mind one great thing why nine members would be better than twelve was because it was almost impossible in years gone by to get a quorum. If they had twelve members the quorum would have to be made larger. What they were there for was to express a wish whether they would have the township divided into three wards or four. To divide it into three was comparatively easy, but to divide it into four, would perplex the committee very greatly indeed. He had not the least doubt the committee which was appointed to go into the question had done their very best, but Haydock did not seem to be made natur- ally for four 'wards, and they had not been very success- ful in their report, but he thought if any others in that ,room had had the task they would not have done any better.—(Hear, hear).—The next thing they had to de- cide was, if they said they would have three wards, whether they would have three members to each ward. or four to each ward. If they wanted any further in- formation he would do his beet to give it--(Applauee). Dr. Dowline, asked if they could have the township divided into :5 wards if neces.eary, and would it net he quite legal to have 15 .nernbers. ' The Chairman : Yes.

Mr. J. Pilling said at the meetings of the Board,whic.ll had been held, they came to the conclusion that they would ask for 12 members, and three wards, which was to be submitted to that meeting, and what they decided the ask he meeting was, whether they would have 9 or 12 members. He thought there was great interest over having 12 members. (Hear, bear.) Mr. Bradshaw moved "That application be in tde to the County Council to divide this township into 3 wards. and 4 members for each ward,and one gal rdiaa for each ward."—The population was now sotnevitere over 7,000, se that if it was necessary to have 9 tnembers, 12 years ago, it was quite necessary to increase it to 12 now. He thought that would be in keeping with the neighbour ing townships, and cited Ciolborne, and Billinze as examples. He did not think they would be asking for anything extravnant in asking for 12 members. (Applause.) Mr. J. Norcross seconded. Mr. J. Walden moved an amendment "that they have 3 wards, with 3 members, and one guardian for each ward." He said the smaller the committees, the better the work would be done , and the better chance there would be of having a quorum, In most of the large Boards, as far as he could see a great deal of work was given out to committees, a sanitary, ward, and finance committee, who would talk over the matters in hand. and submit them to a general committee as a whole, by whom they were gone into again, and if objections were raised to what had been done on the other committees ;I was reported back to them. When there were only 9 members on the Board, most of the committees had al the members on them, and the consevence was •the business would be done in a shorer .pace of time.—l, they had less membura they would get far better men on the Board. (Applause.) Mr. P. Rainford seconded the amendment.

In answer to Mr. Billinge, the Chairman extAained the boundaries of the three wards, and _

Mr. Pilling sta•ed th y found great tit fficelty in mak- ing it into four wards, as they could not get the o. fel- lation equal. He said, in the three wards the population would be practically equal. The Chairman read a statement showing the number of votes in each ward. East ward 383, Central 359, West 444. The only difficulty about those wards was he was afraid when the County Council came to look at it, they would object to the East and Central wards as they stood, and say thew ought to have a smaller population. as that was where the most building would take place ("question"), and the East ward would be next. 'lh- Board were unanimous in thinking that was the best- arrangement that could be made. The rateable value was East ward £8,426; Central £9,435; and West ward £2,755. Rey. J. H. Ridette asked whether there was any reason to suppose that the County Council would sanction a seheme providing for four members per ward. To make it very char, he would ask whether the question had been put. in any form whatever to the County Council, because it was useless for anyone there to vote for Mr. Bradshaw's motion, if they were going to ask for some- thing which was a foregone conclusion. The Chairman said Mr. Matthews told him that the Clerk to the County Council had said that he supposed the Council would pass four menThers to each ward. Rev. J. H. Ridette read a letter he had received from the Clerk to the County Council, saving that four mem- bers to each of three wards could be proposed, but whether the County Council would pass this or not he could not say. An inquiry would have to be held and the matter fully gone into. He wanted to see a scheme providing for 12 members, and in his opinion, no scheme wculd be satisfactory unless it provided for 12 members. Ile drew comparisions between Golberne and Flaydock, in respect to the population and rateable value. He thought the affairs of the township could be looked after better, if they had more members ; they certainly could not be looked after worse. If they had 12 members, there would he 12 people obliged by their offieitil respon- sibilities to see that things were tolexa.bly well managed. (Applause.) The Chairman said if they agreed with Mr. Ridette he would strongly advise them not to put any of the old members of the Haydock Local Board on the Council, as if they could not manage matters beVer than they do now, they should not be put there. (Hear, hear.) Dr. Dowling supported the resolution, providing they got a guarantee that such a thing was possible. From what Mr. Ridette had said, there seemed some sort of a doubt as to whether they could bave four members in each ward. He thought if there was any doubt about the matter, they ought to give the 12 members the bene- fit, and divide the district into four wards. He was strongly in favour of 12 .members ; they had seen what 9 members could do, how capable they were of doing a lot of mischief. (Laughter.) Mr. Taylor was in favour of 9 members. The amendment was then put to the meeting, and defeated by a large majority. The Chairman said the epinion of the meeting was undoubtedly in favour of 12 members, and he would de his utmost to carry out their wishes, fie asked, me Bradshaw whether he was going to stand by his motion, or withdraw it. Mr. Bradshaw pressed his motion, which on being put to the meeting was unanimously carried. A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meeting.

VESTRY MEETING AT HAYDOCK.

The adjourned vestry meeting .ot St. Jfimp,' Church and St. Alban's Mission (nape' was brio in the Church schoolroom, Haydock, on Thursday evening. The Rev. C. M. Bowden, vicar, presided and there were present Rev. J. Andrews (curate), Messrs. A. Evans, E. Jackson,liradshaw, Matthews, and others. The accounts of St. James' and St. Alban's were read by Mr. E. Jackson, and showed an adverse halience of over £33, which had been caused by the thnbers of St. James' Church having to be thoroughly overhauled, in consequence of them shrinking. The accounts were adopted. Votes of thanks to the choir, lay reader (Mr. Walden) and the auditors were accorded.—The Rev. J. Andrews said St. Alhan's during the past 12 months had more than paid its way. He thought that was Ihe first time in its history that it had done iio+. The debt that had accumulated upan it was extended over 14 or 15 years. The work would be very much quickened, if those who were at St. Alnail s were allowed to have a voice in the management of ! it. He was perfectly well aware he shaula be I told that they had a voice through their representa- tive amongst the sidesenee, but they were giving a voAry great handle to people in Black brook to complain, when, out of two wardens, and eight sidesmen, only one lived in Illackbrook. What he suggested was that St. Albaee might be allowed to manage her own affairs subject te a I committee including the Vicar, himself, the two church- wardens, and five men to be elected by the conereeatien who assembled Sunday after Sunday at S. Albans. He would move "that the affairs of St. Albans be managed by a committee consisting of the vicar. curate, chtitafe-1 wardens, and five men elected from St. Albans, as a , temporary ,arraneement for 12 months."—Mr. W. 1 Holland seconded ate motion, which, after some further discussion, was put to the meeting and carried.—Mr. A. Evans suggested that the organist at St. Albans should have a fixed salary.—The Vicar said that would he. a matter for committee. A vote of thanks to the organist and choir of St. Mbions for their services was accorded, and after a similar compliment had been paid to the Chi • man the meeting terminated.

IArI'ESIT NE WS. MR. GLADSTONE.

The" British Medical Journal "says :—Mr. Gladstone's health, which has lately caused considerable uneasiness among his friends, is being gradually restored. The cough no longer troubles him. He eats and sleeps well, and in many ways is better. On Wednesday he was able for the first time lately to join the family party at din- ner. His apparently slow progress is accounted for by the persistence of a symptom which, though not in itself serious, has, as is only natural in a man ot his age,caused some anxiety, and has necessitated rest and perfect quietness. Outing the last few days, however, the trouble appears to have passed away, and hopes are en- tertained that in a day or two he may be able to drive out. As regards the eyes, we believe neither the date nor the place of the proposed operation is yet fixed, but must depend largely on the rate of improvement in his general condition.

===== 0002601_18940428_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COAL TRADE.

A private meeting of the representatives of the Miners' Federation was held in London on Thursday. The ques- tion of the restriction of output was deferred till after the Berlin Congress, which meets next month. Votes of thanks were passed to Mr. Roby,Mr.Cremer,Mr.Pickard, and Mr. Woods for their efforts with regard to the Miners' Eight Hours Bill. It is stated that all the delegates were in favour of a reitriction of output ; but it was con- sidered judicious to await the opinion of the Berlin Con- ference with a view to international action.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

THIMSDAY.-WELSH DISESTABLISHMEN r.

lathe House of Commons on Thursday evening, Mr. ASQUITH, in asking leave to bring in "a bill to terminate the Establishment of the Church of England in Wales and Monmouthshire, and to make provision in respect of the temporalities thereof," said there were a number of parishes belonging to English dioceses which were wholly included geographically in Wales, and, on the other hand,there were a number of parishes belonging to Weleh dioceses which were wholly situated in England. It was proposed to follow the geographical and net the diocesan boundary where the two were not coincident. The date proposed as the date of Disestabluhment was the Ist January, 1896. The right hon. gentleman went at some length into the financial part of the subject and the position of the future Church Representative Body and the Commission which would deal with the tempo- ralities. The Government, he said, proposed to make such provision as would prevent the alienation of and safeguard for every national public purpose the corpus of the property which would pass from the Church to the nation, and that so far as the property was attached to localities and parishes it should in future be applied and enjoyed locally and parochially. All the churches except the cathedrals would, on the application of the Representative Body of the disestab lished Church, be vested by the Commissioners he that- body, subject to all public and private rights. The cathe- drals, the Government thought, ought to be treated as national monuments, but on a request from the Repres- entative Body it would be the duty of the Commissioners to permit them to be continued to be used for the pur- poses of divine service. The parsonage-houses would be handed over to the Representative Body. The die- established church would retain the private benefactors —goirns back to 1703,—which amounted to about £13,000 a year. The burial grounds would be vested in ' the parish councils, district councils, and borough coun- cils, and, subject to.the interests of existing incumbents the glebes would be similarly vested, and the tithe rent would be vested in the County Council. As to existing interests, they proposed that so long as the holder of any benefice or office performed the duties of the office he slsould be entitled to his present emoluments.with the alternative of exchanging his existing interest for a compensation annuity on a lower scale. Curates were aot dealt with, and as to - private patrons compensation was not to exceed one year's emoluments of the benefice. Sir. M. Hicze-Bissca said disendowtnent spelt the fome thing as plunder and sacrilege. This was not a //letter that effected Wales alone ; it vitally affected the Church of England. Sir G. Osborne Morgan advised the sup- porters ef the Church to pausr, befote they rejected the bill, for it was possible they might go turther and fare worse. Mr. ABEL Moires said it was a good bill, and would be welcomed by the people interested. Mr. Addison and Sir R. Webster took part in the debate, which, oa the motion of Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE, was adjourned, Monday being fixect for its resumption.

In the House of Lords the Lord CHANCELLOR moved the second reading of the Law of Inheritance Amend- ment Bill, the main object of which was to provide that the property of intestates should be dealt with in the same manner whether it was real or per4onal. The Duke of Norfolk moved the rejection of the bill,and was supported by the Marquis of Salisbury. The EIJI of Rosebery " warned their Lordships in their own inter- ests not to reject this very m,glerate tneasure." Loot Halsbury said he did nr t regard the bill as of inueli im- portance. Ho believed it was brou4ltt forwarcl to afford an additional cry against the House of Lfirds at the general election. The motion for the second reading was defeated by 63 to 52.
